A.21. Executive summary (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

While the proportion of renewable energy (RE) generation is rising every year, it remains a small percentage of the total world-wide electricity production and far from the targets needed to meet the Paris Agreement objectives. The proportion of the world’s electricity generated by wind, solar – together with hydroelectricity – rose from 6 percent in 2010 to 12.9 percent in 2018. Large solar and wind energy deployment is needed for the world to be on track to meet its commitments under the Paris Agreement and achieve the Sustainable Development Goals by 2030, according to the Sustainable Development Scenario of the International Energy Agency (World Energy Outlook 2016). 950 GW of solar PV and 580 GW of wind need to be installed by 2025 in order for the world to meet its commitments under the Paris Agreement.

The limited installed capacity of cost-competitive solar and wind in developing countries reveals important regulatory, structural, and technical constraints. The main constraints identified are (i) limitations on access to finance, particularly for small-scale projects, which hampers the mobilization of sustainable private investments; (ii) limited procurement capacity to select the infrastructure needed to ensure the reliability and quality of on-takers and (v) VRE grid integration challenges due to weak grids especially in Sub-Saharan Africa.

The World Bank through its Energy Sector Management Assistance Program (ESMAP), developed the Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative (SRII) to address these challenges and promote the development of cost-competitive renewable energy projects. The SRII is a multi-donor initiative that aims at reducing the risks associated with RE investments in developing countries, thereby increasing their attractiveness for private sector investment.

<sup>1</sup> For the purposes of the loans and grants, the Executing Entities (EEs) would be the Borrowers/Recipients of the loans/grants. For the purposes of the guarantees contemplated under Component 3.2, the EEs would be the private sector RE project companies.


---
**Page 7**
---

24 under the leadership of the World Bank in partnership with the Agence Française de Développement (AFD), the International Renewable Energy Agency (IRENA) and the International Solar Alliance (ISA). SRMI – previously known as the Solar Risk Mitigation Initiative – aims to support countries in developing and implementing a national solar energy strategy. The SRMI Facility is designed to unlock private sector investments to reduce reliance on public sector financing while maximizing socio-economic benefits for the country.

Under the context of the Covid-19 crisis, SRMI’s unique, integrated and replicable approach can support countries unlock a green economic stimulus through targeted public investments in order to achieve three main objectives: (i) accelerate the deployment of solar energy projects; (ii) provide for (i) technical assistance to help countries develop evidence-based VRE targets, implement a sustainable renewable energy program, and maintain robust procurement processes with transaction advisors; (ii) critical financial support to help countries overcome immediate liquidity constraints in order to increase access to electricity; and (iii) risk mitigation instruments to cover residual risks perceived by private investors. The SRMI Facility is designed to be flexible enough to adapt its approach to the evolving economic situation alongside government economic support measures such as (short-term job allowances, direct cash transfers to citizens or targeted liquidity support to small and medium size enterprises).

The SRMI Facility will leverage GCF resources to provide technical assistance and grants to support seven initial countries (Phase 1), namely Botswana, Central African Republic (CAR), Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Kenya, Mali, Namibia and Uzbekistan. If in 2017 those countries represented 1.2 percent of the world’s population but accounted for 35% of global poverty, they also represent a significant share of the world’s energy demand. The seven countries are currently on track to triple their emissions between 2017 and 2030. With international support, the seven countries are committed to reducing their per capita emissions by at least half in order to align with the Paris Agreement’s main contributing sector. However, the countries have now prioritized immediate emergency response to manage the current Covid-19 crisis. The question of how the economic recovery is designed remains critical for these countries and will determine whether they are able to achieve their climate goals or not.

It is therefore critical in this context to support policymakers to develop green stimulus Interventions that can help them recover from the pandemic while also contributing to the achievement of the SDGs. The SRMI Facility, through the seven projects, is expected to contribute to the shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways as follows:

- **Directly support the integration of an estimated 2.5 GW of RE**, (i) 0.6 GW of solar PV, (ii) 1.8GW of wind power and (iii) 0.1GW of hydro power plants. The 400 MW of solar PV projects are expected to be built during the lifetime of the project and 400 MW expected to be built after the end of the project’s operation period. The 250 MW of wind power projects are expected to be built in the first five years of the project, around US$1 billion in public investment of which USD 250 million of GCF would need to have been disbursed. These public investment projects will also enable the integration of another 1.6GW of RE through the integration of other projects is enabled due to the VRE integration analysis and grid investments.
- **Directly reduce greenhouse emissions by an estimated 97.35 million tons CO2e**, mostly from this intervention for a period of 10 years, with smaller contributions from wind power plants and 8.96m metric tons of CO2 for 4.0 GW of RE power plants over the 25 years of their operation that are expected to be prepared under the SRMI Facility projects but not built during the lifetime of the project.

Unlock an estimated USD 3.3 billion of private investments, including USD 3 billion for the 2.1 GW built during the lifetime of the projects and USD 300 million for the 0.4 GW built after the end of the projects.

- Provide access to clean low-emission electricity to an estimated 4.2 million people through the electrification investments.
- Strengthen institutional and regulatory systems for planning and development.
- Reduce lock-in long-lived, climate-vulnerable infrastructure by increasing resilience of grid infrastructure.

The SRIII Facility aims to be scaled up to a total USD 400 million in GCF funding amount by including additional countries (namely Indonesia and Vietnam), which have shown interest and extensive experience in the sector to date. These are expected to submit into NDCs for both two countries post submission of this funding proposal. Vietnam and Indonesia are expected to have projects that meet the agreed eligibility criteria as defined in the present funding proposal. The Phases 1 and 2 of the SRIII Facility will be complementary, with the following additional information: (i) avoid a total of 286.32 m metric tons of CO₂, including 60.6 m metric tons of CO₂ during project lifetime; and (ii) leverage a total of USD 10.2 bl, including USD 1.2 bl during the project lifetime and USD 9 bl built after the end of the project. Once and if approved by GCF Board, Phase Z will be complementary to the present Funding Proposal.

B. PROGRAM INFORMATION

### B.1. Climate Action (max. 1000 words, approximately two pages)

The SBRM Facility aims at deploying the SBRM approach to unlock RE deployment by leveraging sustainable private investments starting with seven countries, namely Botswana, CAR, DRC, Kenya, Mali, Namibia and Uzbekistan. These countries were selected based on the willingness and interest of national governments to implement the SBRM approach in their NDCs and low-emissions development pathways. In addition, those countries represent a large sample of developing countries – with around 200 million inhabitants – facing different levels of economic development, from least developed countries (LDC) heavily dependent on domestic fossil fuels, and fragile and post conflict countries to medium income countries. The lessons learnt from the implementation of SBRM in those countries will enable to replicate the approach at scale in a classified set of affluently developing countries, including some of the largest emitters such as Indonesia or particular SGD 13 on Climate Action and SGD 7 on Affordable and Clean Energy.

Under the context of the Covid-19 crisis, the SBRM Facility’s projects aim to support countries in their economic recovery. The current global economic situation is a unique moment in time where the economic recovery design will determine whether the Paris Agreement targets can be achieved. In that regard, macro-level economic stimulus packages need to be combined with catalytic climate finance for specific projects and sectors. This will allow to achieve the 2030 NDC targets while reducing the share of total global GHG emissions under a BAU scenario they would on average triple their emissions between now and 2050, compared to current levels. The SBRM Facility aims at achieving 10% CO2e emissions reductions. As per their NDCs presented in Figure 1 Botswana, CAR, DRC, Kenya, Mali, Namibia and Uzbekistan will commit to reduce, conditionally or externally financing with the exception of Botswana, conditional on receiving external funding from the SBRM Facility. The countries’ unconditional targets are 89 percent and 10 percent, respectively. However, the priority of the countries is the immediate emergency response to the current Covid-19 crisis. The question of how the economic recovery is designed remains crucial for achieving the Paris Agreement targets. If the economic stimulus packages are not well-designed, it will be achieved. If government do not roll out low carbon development strategies and policies in response to the coming economic crisis, emissions could increase by 2030 and even overshoot previously project levels by 2030, despite lower economic growth in the period to 2030.

It will be essential in this context to support policymakers to develop green stimulus interventions with targeted public investments in order to unlock large private investments for economic recovery.

According to the IEA and estimations by the World Bank, around 20 GW of RE and 45 GW of RE (including large hydropower) are needed just to meet the current demand for electricity. This is equivalent to about half of their electricity mix to reach the 2°C targets and universal access. This represents around USD 15 billion in investments needed just for new solar and wind generation by 2030. To reach this objective, large amounts of private sector investment will be required.

The seven countries have been receiving support from other multilateral development banks (MDB) and bilateral banks to deploy RE in their countries outside of SRII and of the scope of the present Fund proposal. The aim is to ensure that these projects are complementary at a national level to ensure complementarity of programs, avoid overlaps and enhance synergies. More precisely:

a. Botswana: The new World Bank-supported solar program is aligned with current support provided by Power Africa (USAID) for the development of a 10 MW solar plant (MWE) on solar farms.

b. CAR: MDBs and donors are supporting their input in CAR – which includes access to, and access and generation; (i) The World Bank is financing an ongoing project consisting of a 25 MW solar plant with 25 MWh battery storage – the PURACLE project, (ii) ADB is currently financing the extension of Boali’s existing 10 MW solar plant (MWE) to 30 MW and the construction of a 40 MW solar plant between Bebou and Bangui to 110 kV. (iii) ADB is working on refurbishing the existing 10 MV of Boali solar plant (MWE), (iv) The World Bank is supporting the development of a 25 MW solar plant, and eventually the construction of Boali’s hydropower plant (3 MW) and (v) A Saudi fund is supporting investment in thermal generation for Bangui (10 MW). The new World Bank grant-funded project aims to support the development of a 40 MW solar plant with 25 MWh battery storage, as well as towards mobilization of private investments and such, while supporting further deployment of disruptive technologies such as battery storage – CAR having the largest battery storage projects being built in Africa.

c. DRC: The World Bank has been financing investment and technical assistance in DRC focusing on grid-connection, private investment mobilization for electrification, technical and financial support to the utility company (EDM) and the development of a national RE strategy. EDM is currently implementing the current portfolio of other MDBs such as ADB, AFDB, United Nations Development Program (UNDP), Japan International Cooperation Agency (JICA), the UK Department for International Development (DFID), etc.

d. Kenya: The World Bank has been working on last mile electrification, generation (mostly geothermal) and utility company strengthening. The government is currently in the process of a large-scale auction of wind energy. Since 2016, the World Bank Group, AFD and EIB have supported solar and wind development with AFD financing a strategy on solar auction. The solar auction program has not yet been implemented due to limited appetite of the utility. However, the government has requested the World

e. Mali: The World Bank energy portfolio in Mali is focusing on access, sector strengthening and new generation. As part of its support to the West Africa Power Pool (WAPP), the World Bank is supporting the development of a regional electricity market in West Africa. This includes coordination with other MDBs that are also supporting the utility and the sector name, namely ADB, the Islamic Development Bank (IsDB), the European Union, the West African Development Bank (as a finance partner to the WAPP), and the African Development Bank Group. The World Bank is working on specific projects in the electricity sector in Mali and working in partnership on supporting the deployment of solar generation – with the World Bank leading the support to the VRE grid-upgrade investment project, ADB supporting the development of the 100 MW solar power plant, and the EU’s PV transmission line from Bamako to Mamou, Kayes in Senegal, and Kigali to Mauritania is funded by the AFD. The World Bank is also working on a number of projects that are focused on electrification in rural remote areas; the AfDB is preparing a rural electrification program for some 60 localities; and the AfDB, AFD, and BOAD are co-financing the Guine-Mali interconnector with the World Bank. GCF funding is also being sought to support the development of solar generation projects in Mali.

f. Namibia: The World Bank-supported solar project is aligned with current support provided by Power Africa and the AfDB under a MOU on solar deployment. KfW and GIZ have also been supporting NamPower – the utility – in developing their least-cost generation plans and grid analysis as well as a battery storage pilot. Proxima provided debt financing for the 37 MWp PV. The Deutsche Gesellschaft für Internationale Zusammenarbeit (GIZ) is providing technical assistance with other the MDBs and Development Finance Institutions (DFIs), the World Bank is coordinating its RE support to ensure complementarity of the different work streams.

g. Uzbekistan: The World Bank Group has been very active in solar deployment and such, with the support of ADB, KfW, and AfDB. The 100 MW solar PV project was implemented with the support of the World Bank Group Scaling Solar Program with a 1 Gw of solar PV target. Asian Development Bank (ADB) is supporting the development of the RE sector in Uzbekistan by supporting new deployment in the country. Strong coordination is ongoing to ensure that the lending and grant-based risk allocation. The new World Bank-supported VRE program in Uzbekistan will enable the integration of the VRE being deployed under the three MDBs support.

Outside of the scope of this document, it should be noted that efforts to bolster the development of the Initiative’s core objectives have led to the establishment of a multi-stakeholder group. The multi-stakeholder group comprises of ADB, KfW, and AfDB in addition to AFD, IRENA, and EBRD. This multi-stakeholder group is an important step forward for the Initiative as it will provide a platform for sharing work each stakeholder’s vision to support the development of renewables in developing countries. A high-level collaboration with the Initiative stakeholders is ongoing (outside of the present Finance Committee) on how best to align their respective strategies and programs, including through the SEFA initiative on developing RE aligned with SRM’s principles, and AfD’s work program on RE

# B.2. Theory of change (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages plus diagram)

The SRM Facility aims at tackling the lack of sustainable and bankable pipeline of RE projects in developing countries to achieve low-emission development pathways. The SRM theory of change is for the entire Programme and draws on the lessons learned from the successes and failures of national climate funds and other international initiatives.

## a. Development risks

- **grid risk**, including connection risks;
- **land risk**, including availability, permitting, and environmental and social (E&S) aspects;
- **legal risk**, including the applicable regulatory, dispute resolution, and judicial frameworks;
- **procurement risk**; and
- **integrity risk**.

## b. Operational risks

- **administrative risk** (including the off-taker's record of performance and timely payment) and risk of contract termination;
- the country’s power sector risk (including sector financial sustainability risk, reform risk, regulatory risk, and risk associated with government construction work);
- market risk (including currency risk and interest rate risk);
- **country and macroeconomic risk**; and
- political risk (including risk of expropriation or contract; exportation; transfer restriction and currency inconvertibility; and war and civil disturbance).

To tackle these risks, the SRM Facility developed activities around four components – that are adapted to the needs of each country as part of the Facility, namely:

### a. Component 1: Technical Assistance - concentrates on (i) comprehensive and integrated planning support for RE projects, including grid integration and VRE integration analysis and dispatch and planning capacity building; (ii) legal, financial and procurement aspects capacity building and transaction advisory support to implement competitive and transparent tendering of priority, financially feasible RE projects; and (iii) E&S capacity building at local level (E&S) studies for solar wind parks for future tenders.


---
**Page 14**
---

b. **Component 2: Public Investments** – concentrates on public investments for (i) solar and/or wind park commercialized infrastructure, (ii) VRE integration grid upgrades (including publicly-owned battery storage) and enhancing grid resilience to climate change, and (iii) electrification to increase population access.

c. **Component 3: Risk Mitigation Instruments** – concentrates on providing risk mitigation instruments to private investors for grid-connected and off-grid renewable energy/storage projects; and

d. **Component 4: Project Management** – concentrates on the management of the projects and coordination and implementation of their activities, in compliance with the Executing Entities (EEs) and Program Implementing Entities (PIEs) contractual obligations included or referred to in the legal agreements.

The technical assistance component focuses on the country/micro level risks looking at (i) developing sustainable RE targets to reduce planning risks and provide evidence-based targets for low-emission development strategies, (ii) reducing project risks through a robust and transparent selection of IPPs; and (iii) increasing government’s institutional capacity to manage the implementation of renewable energy projects and permits through solar/wind park schemes.

The public investment component focuses on the grid and infrastructure level risks looking at (i) ensuring grid stability in response to the grid stability due to high penetration of VRE, (ii) increasing grid resilience to climate change impacts, finance the public infrastructure needed for the IPPs; and (iii) providing access to electricity to vulnerable populations.

The risk mitigation instrument component focuses on the residual risks for private investors looking at ensuring a sustainable leveraging of private investments in grid-connected and off-grid RE projects.

The main expected outcomes under the SRMI Facility are:
- a reduction in GHG emissions thanks to an increase in investments in VRE capacity through private investments in grid-connected and off-grid projects, and enabled VRE integration,
- the leveraging of private investments reducing the burden on public sector finances,
- improved access for rural communities along the projects, and
- strengthening of institutional and regulatory capacity.

The SRMI Facility's objective is to support eligible countries to shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways and increase access to affordable, reliable, sustainable and modern energy to its populations.

# B.3 Project Description (max. 2000 words, approximately 4 pages)

## Overall Background

Solar and wind energy generation, as part of a green stimulus package post-covid-19, could become game changers as they are already least-cost source of generation in most countries and are modular with shorter construction time than other power plants. Solar and wind energy generation also have the potential to provide (i) access to electricity for those who currently lack energy access as it is a module source of energy; (iv) responding to fast growing demand as they have short construction times, which can be used in response to economic recovery and infrastructure development (including from women empowerment's perspective). Moreover, solar and wind help countries deal with their potential power shortages while ensuring they are not dependent on imported fuels.

However, large penetration of VRE is usually rapidly constrained due to grid technical limitations and lack of flexibility in the grids. The main challenges related to the integration of VRE into the grid are its variability and limited capacity to maintain grid stability as it can only produce power during the day and when the wind is blowing. Three issues must be considered: (i) the need for sufficient VRE and grid capacity for the generation mix to meet demand at any hour of the year, considering VRE variability; (ii) the economic costs associated with integrating VRE into the grid, which can be reduced significantly thanks to VRE and the investment costs required for deploying and integrating VRE; and (iii) the limitations of solar(PV/wind capacity to maintain grid stability due to its variability and limited capacity to provide ancillary services. The need for sufficient VRE and grid capacity is also important as a sustainable penetration of VRE can be constrained due to (i) technical limitations, i.e. the grid have the disruption of power quality operation, reliability, and security; and (ii) economic limitations, i.e. even if there is a grid with many flexible generators such as hydropower, they may not be able to provide flexibility for the grid as ancillary services may not be priced in their power purchase agreement (PPA). This can lead to "take or pay" arrangements pushing the utility to dispatch them prior to considering new renewables.

Investment for grid modernization and upgrade will be required to ensure large penetration of VRE in the seven countries. Taking advantage of variable renewable generation requires significant investment in the grid, including transmission lines, substations, and other infrastructure to support the gradual transition of power systems into "VRE-friendly" grids that will significantly reduce integration costs and improve system reliability. This will require a long-term vision for planning and management to adapt to the particular characteristics of VRE. It also requires better forecasting methods and stringent grid code requirements. In addition, this may provide the opportunity for the country to also look at other renewable energy sources such as geothermal.

If the technical aspects are critical, the deployment schemes selected to mobilize and select IPPs are also crucial. Since 2015, most countries that moved into competitive selection of IPPs directly benefited from a reduction in solar construction and equipment, and is cost efficient through lower PPA prices compared to competitive bidding under FIT or other procurement mechanisms. As of the end of this Funding Proposal, Vietnam, Kenya and Mongolia have FIT prices of US$ 9.35 cents, US$ 12 cents and US$ 8.70 cents per kWh respectively; while Ethiopia, Mozambique, Namibia, Rwanda, South Africa, Tanzania, Zambia and Philippines announced PPA prices of USD 2.44, 2.56, USD 3.9 and USD 4.5 cents per kWh, respectively. The main difference between FIT and competitive bidding is that under FIT, Governments are fixing the price themselves while in a competitive bidding process the private investors are

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 16 OF 72

## B

### Under competitive bidding, governments can access lower prices as IPPs will reflect in their proposed PPA price.

- (i) the capital expenditure (CAPEX) cost at the time of the bid - since 2010 CAPEX in wind and solar decreased rapidly, in particular solar module cost and it is difficult to assess its cost a few years ahead.
- (ii) the operational expenditure (OPEX) cost at the time of the bid - since 2010 OPEX in wind and solar decreased rapidly as well.

### Bankable contractual arrangements with fair risk allocation between the private and public stakeholders that enable IPPs to attract long-term non-limited recourse project finance that lower the cost of capital, are recommended. Based on the identified development challenges and in particular grid curtailment risks for VRE, two different approaches to reduce the development risks perceived by IPPs and ensure low-cost competitive bidding, are recommended. Both schemes aim to reduce the development risks perceived by IPPs, thus reducing the risk premium in the cost of capital and reduce the utility's cost of integrating solar generation into the grid. The main outcome expected is a reduced IPPs tariff proposed by developers. More precisely:

- Under the substantive curtailment approach, the government sets a target of solar capacity to be procured at a certain time. A defined amount of unsubsidized MW capacity, and a certain MW capacity at each substation/federer is opened for bidding to achieve the target. IPPs complete through a competitive bidding process to obtain the right to connect their solar PV/wind projects to the grid. The government will conduct a selection criteria comprising technical, financial and safeguard aspects. IPPs are responsible for connecting the solar PV/wind installation to the grid and develop RE in selected areas. It will enable the government to reduce the development risks perceived by IPPs through the potential curtailment and/or additional costs to integrate the solar PV and wind projects as well as actively drive grid investments needed for new VRE generation.

- The solar/wind park concept, with pre-approved comprehensive-devising measures (including land, power transmission lines, etc.) in place, is proposed to reach fully cost-effective electricity tariff. The government identifies project site(s), conducts land clearance, and constructs shared infrastructure for the solar/wind park that can range from the evacuation line to basic grid connection. IPPs will be responsible for connecting their solar PV/wind projects to the grid as well as conducting and operation of the solar/wind energy project. This scheme is critical when IPPs are identifying risk of curtailment and land ownership issues as paramount to the deployment of solar/wind energy.

### RE deployment can also be an opportunity for countries to maximize socio-economic benefits which is particularly important in the Covid-19 context. The lack of visibility on the local opportunities, the tight timelines for project development, and the limited understanding of the renewable energy potential of the local industries do not encourage the bidders to explore what could be done competitively in a given country. Assessing the positioning of the local players on the solar/wind value chain and identifying their role in the RE projects will increase their chances to benefit from the opportunities offered by the new RE projects. Factoring these measures in an RE roadmap and in the bidding processes launched can increase the participation of local players in a competitive manner, fostering the transfer of knowledge and the skills


---
**Page 19**
---

development and allowing them to build a track record to the extent possible. In addition, expanding the RE roadmap to address the needs of the local communities around the RE projects implemented and to design adequate programs to increase their resilience and enhance their livelihoods will ensure that they effectively benefit from the RE projects. The RE projects are implemented in areas where women live and large plots of lands are usually located in areas where the level of living is below the national average. The local communities there face numerous challenges hindering their development, including tailor-made programs to increase their resilience and enhance their livelihoods.

These programs aim to maximize the socio-economic benefits of the RE projects from the local industry as well as from the local community perspectives will pay a special attention to women empowerment. Employment opportunities created by large-scale solar and wind infrastructure projects often benefit men more than women, especially in rural areas where large projects are developed. In the remote areas where large projects are developed, women, if employed, are more exposed to lower-paying jobs with less security of employment. As a result, without a proactive policy to promote female employment throughout the RE project-cycle, there is a risk that women will miss out on new employment opportunities across the RE value chain.

The World Bank SRMI aims at addressing in a comprehensive manner the different challenges that are faced by countries implementing privately financed RE and the associated socio-economic benefits. Launched in 2018 for the COP 24 under the leadership of the World Bank in partnership with the AFD, IRENA and ISA, SRMI aims to support countries in developing sustainable solar and wind energy programs (SWEPs) that are aligned with their national development plans or financing, while maximizing the socio-economic benefits triggered by the projects deployed. Its unique and integrated approach is based on three pillars: (i) risk mitigation instruments to help countries develop evidence-based RE targets, implement a sustainable renewable energy program, and set up and maintain transparent and competitive procurement processes with transaction advisors; (ii) critical enabling factors for scaling up RE deployment in developing countries, including increasing access to electricity; and (iii) risk mitigation instruments to cover residual risks perceived by private investors. Its framework is supporting the creation of jobs, the development of skills as well as increasing access to energy while enhancing the livelihoods of the local communities around the projects.

SRMI aims to support countries to:
- develop sustainable RE programs to leverage private investment at scale for grid-connected and off-grid RE projects,
- implement SRMI’s RE programs through transparent and robust competitive tenders,
- improve their grid capacity to integrate VRE and resilience to climate change,
- leverage private investments for RE grid-connected and off-grid projects,
- build a strong enabling environment,
- avoid GHG emissions, and
- maximize the socio-economic benefits triggered by the RE projects developed.

## Country Context

The SRMI approach aims to support countries in a comprehensive multi-dimensional risk mitigation approach, yet in a flexible manner adapting to their current situation and constraints. Therefore, it is critical to understand the different constraints and starting points of each of the countries currently under consideration.

Botswana's power sector currently relies on high-carbon electricity, largely produced from coal at the Monipole power station using the country’s large coal reserves (around 200 billion tons). To mitigate this reliance, Botswana has 30 gigawatts of potential renewable energy capacity (74.5 percent coal) and the SAPP market (74.5 percent coal), contributing to coal-fired emissions and unnecessary fiscal outflows as tariffs do not cover peak power prices. Only sixty percent of national households have access to electricity, with a target of 100% by 2030. Botswana aims for universal access by 2030. Under a BAU scenario, Botswana could see increased output of coal-fired power generation capacity from the Monipole power station rising to be 2.5 times its current level by 2030. Yet, Botswana enjoys one of the highest irradiation densities in the world, with over 3,200 hours of sunshine per year and an average global irradiation throughout the country. This resource is complemented by a wind resource, which can be harnessed for both onshore and offshore solar. Botswana also benefits from a good wind resource, complementary with the solar resource, with a potential capacity estimated between 300 MW and 500 MW. No solar/wind projects are built to date.

-CARD's electricity sector is characterized by inadequate infrastructure, a weak policy and regulatory framework and a utility that is struggling to recover costs and thus maintain and expand services. The overall rate of access is 8 percent in the country. Total installed capacity is 32 MW, of which 10 MW are solar PV systems. The total demand for electricity is estimated at around 60 MW. The majority of the generation is hydropower and diesel plants. Therefore, to improve reliability in the power system, support stability, and promote


---
**Page 21**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 | PAGE 19 OF 72

B

preambulating in a context of fragility, the country is in dire need of sizeable and urgent increases in its electricity generation capacity. Solar with battery storage is being sought as a solution with a World Bank financed utility-owned solar plant under construction and a deployment of solar-hydro systems in the near future. The current law does not have any prior utility-owned generation but has a provision for IPPs in its new Electricity Law and started working on the decrees for the implementation of the law.

- DRC has an access rate of around 9 percent. The well-distributed potential of both hydro and solar resources is yet to be harnessed due to lack of infrastructure. The country has several large hydropower plants over the last decades, leaving most of the country's population unserved. SNEC, the national state-owned utility, has a critical role to play in access, in particular for rural electrification. However, the company is facing financial difficulties from its deteriorating assets and mounting debt that hinders its ability to drive electrification outside of Kinshasa. Therefore, it will be critical for the future of the electrification in DRC to develop large solar-hydro systems. In order to attract private sector investment as well as private public investments, the government must improve its institutional and regulatory framework.

- After decades of shortages, Kenya now has excess generation capacity to meet its demand, and supply is predominantly from renewable energy sources. The current installed generation capacity in Kenya is 20 GW with a total installed capacity of over 30 percent. Renewable energy (geothermal and hydro) account for 65 percent of total installed capacity and almost 90 percent of total electricity generation. Geothermal energy contributes to about 18 percent of the country's electricity production, making Kenya one of the global leaders in use of this firm, renewable resource. Kenya has a FIT since 2008, and revised in 2012, for wind, small-scale hydro, geothermal, biogas and solar with a standardized FIT. However, little solar and wind generation were built under the FIT, with only 65 MW and 339 MW respectively.

- Despite significant progress in recent years, Kenya's electricity sector remains fragmented and poorly regulated. The state utility KENGEN has an expired thermal power generation (heavy fuel oil (HFO) and diesel) and have an access rate of around 39 percent. The development of interconnections with its neighboring countries is enabling the country to reduce its cost of electricity, but it also increases its vulnerability to external shocks. Kenya has been bilaterally negotiating with IPs for new solar projects. No solar/wind projects are built to date as prices negotiated are well above the regional standards (with prices well above USD 0.10 per kWh). The government is currently revising the FIT and the renewable energy policy, and therefore, will face issues with integrating large amounts of VRE without any critical investments put in place.

- Namibia imports a significant portion of its electricity needs and is particularly reliant on South Africa's imports. These imports also lead to high foreign exchange, high electricity costs which place a burden on the balance of payments and budget, and vulnerable electricity supply. The government has taken an important policy decision to increase domestic supply and reduce electricity import dependency by 2035. Namibia aims at achieving 80 percent self-sufficiency in electricity generation and transmission. Namibia aims to achieve 80 percent self-sufficiency through domestic electricity production by 2022. Namibia is one of the best countries in the world for CSP, PV and wind production. Namibia has an experience in FIT for very small VRE projects.

and a limited experience in competitive bidding. Namibia has an installed 120 MW of PV capacity and 5 MWf of wind.

### Uzbekistan's energy system is characterized by high losses and low reliability of supply due in part to the rapidly aging supply infrastructure that was mostly constructed during the Soviet era.
The energy sector is highly dependent on natural gas, which is a major source of commodity exports. The country has a limited capacity for renewable energy generation and transmission. Uzbekistan needs to seek sustainable energy transition pathways. Natural gas accounts for 96 percent of the total primary energy supply in Uzbekistan (2015). In addition to this, Uzbekistan has a large coal reserve base, and more than 80 percent of the total installed capacity of 12.5 GW in Uzbekistan is natural gas based, followed by hydropower (16 per cent) and coal (11 percent). The least-cost power generation expansion scenario for Uzbekistan shows that the country will continue to rely on natural gas as its main fuel source accounting for around 30 percent of the total capacity mix (7.5 GW). Uzbekistan has already a very successful tendering system in place with the support of the World Bank Group Solar Solar that was launched in 2014. The government is currently working to expand this model in order to reach their ambitious VRE targets, Uzbekistan needs to tackle the limitation on its grid availability flexibility which is expected once a higher penetration of VRE will be reached.

### Programme's Objective & Components

The SRMI Facility’s objective is to support the eligible countries to shift to low-emission sustainable development pathways by providing them with affordable technologies, sustainable and modern energy in their portfolios. The SRMI Facility is developed around four components that are complementary.
Depending on the country specifics such as access rate, available plans and grid flexibility, the SRMI Facility will provide a tailored package of support for each country. The identified risk mitigant that would enable to reduce the cost of RE deployment, contributing to low-emission development pathway. An individual project level, the Facility development objectives will be adjusted to reflect the specific characteristics of the project and which objective and impacts the activities in each project are particularly designed to focus on.

The Facility and its Components have been developed with enough flexibility so as to be able to cater to the needs of different countries. The SRMI Facility’s components as well as its instruments are applicable to all countries that aim to apply the SRMI approach to sustainably move forward with their energy transition.

### Component 1: Technical Assistance

Grant amounts will be allocated by the EE (Host Country) to the financing of the following types of activities to be carried out by their Ministry or state utility responsible for energy, on an as country-needed basis:

#### Sub-Component 1.1 Generation plans with VRE Integration and Capacity Building (technical)
- Preparation of a new national (or adaptation of an existing) least-cost power generation plan, which includes reflective VRE integration analyses based on technical and commercial constraints, an evaluation of the dispatch capabilities and the limits of grid integration and determination of the capacity expansion needs for generation at an EE level.
- Preparation of a new national (or adaptation of an existing) least-cost transmission/distribution plan, which includes capacity expansion models simulating generation and transmission capacity

B

Investment, given assumptions about future electricity demand, fuel prices, technology cost and performance, policy and regulations, and providing informed solar targets and indication of where best to locate future projects as well as a list of key investments needed to improve grid VRE integration.

- Provision of institutional capacity building support to government institutions and/or utilities (including through training by the EE) for energy planning and dispatch (including using grid-connected battery storage).

## Sub-component 1.2: Transaction Advisory and Capacity Building (procurement, legal)

- Provision of technical assistance (including legal, procurement and transaction advisory services)
- Implementation of capacity building activities to support:
    - The assessment of the existing legal framework for renewable energy projects
    - Development of a suitable financing scheme that is conducive to private sector investment.
    - Design of energy projects including key terms of the PPA and government support agreements (including stakeholder and community engagement, and potential investors).
    - Selection of IPPs and mini-grid developers.
    - Negotiations of the final contractual arrangements for the selected IPPs and mini-grid developers.

## Sub-component 1.3: Solar and wind park preparatory studies

- Preparation of preparatory and feasibility studies and environmental and social risk mitigation instruments, for the development of solar PV, solar PV with battery storage, CSP, onshore wind and offshore wind projects (including selection of IPPs and mini-grid developers) selection process.
- Preparatory and feasibility studies may include, as needed, geotechnical and hydrological studies, socio-economic studies, environmental and social analyses, site-specific grid interconnection studies, solar irradiation/measurement analyses and other studies as needed.

## Component 2: Public Investment

Loan amounts (or grant for grant only countries) will be allocated by the EE (Host Country) to the financing of the following types of activities to be carried out by their Ministry or state utility responsible for energy, on an as-country-needed basis:

### Sub-component 2.1: Public investments in solar and wind park infrastructure

- Carry out public sector investments in support of the development of solar and wind parks such as acquisition/leasehold, construction of transmission lines and fencing of such infrastructure for solar and wind parks.

### Sub-component 2.2: Public investments for VRE integration grid upgrades and grid resilience

- Investment for grid modernization and upgrade to improve grid's flexibility to integrate large amounts of VRE, limit curtailment or grid instability and adapt to climate change related shocks.

## B

### Including power system planning, grid management, strengthening of forecasting methods and grid code requirements.
Grid reinforcements that support VRE integration may include (i) addition or replacement of lines and transformers for grid expansion and capacity enhancements (both for accessing new generation as well as for connecting VRE to the grid), (ii) solutions to voltage and frequency issues, such as capacitor banks, battery storage and other reactive power compensations, together with flexible alternating current transmission systems, (iii) equipment for faster and more reliable fault clearing, (iv) advanced metering infrastructure, (v) improved forecasting systems, and (vi) automats for controlling generation units and grid operations through automatic control systems. Some of these technologies which commercially viability may not have been established yet such as load shifting battery storage.

### Sub-Component 2.3: Public investments for electrification
- Investments as needed to support increased electrification, in partnership with the private sector when feasible, including through solar home systems, mini-grids and grid connection, as best appropriate in each country, with a particular focus on women.

## Component 3: Risk Mitigation Instruments

### Sub-Component 3.1: Risk mitigation instruments for solar/battery mini-grids and/or off-grid private investments
Provision of financial instruments (in the form of guarantees or reimbursable grants whose reimbursement is conditional and modular based on verified performance, or other instruments derived from their financial engineering) to reduce the following risks of investors or their commercial financiers and enhance financial sustainability, in the following areas:

- **Reimbursement of renewable grant to mitigate the critical risks related to private capital investment in the off-grid space (mini grids and/or SHS – for which Multi-Tier Frameworks (MTF) data will be used as a basis for assessment) as far as government stake risk affecting the investors for the first few years of operation and consequently financial risks affecting pricing and terms of financing available to the investors.**

### Sub-Component 3.2: Risk mitigation instruments for grid-connected RE projects
- Provision of measures to mitigate the critical risks related to private capital investment and provision of guarantees or other forms of risk mitigation instruments, as needed, including those taken as needed to leverage private sector investments in grid-connected RE projects. If GCF so approves, these risk mitigation instruments may be provided without member country counter-guarantees. The terms and conditions for such arrangements will be determined following the occurrence of a covered risk would not be reimbursed to the AE or to GCF.

### Component 4: Project Management
Management of the projects and coordination and implementation of their activities, in compliance with the EEs and PIEs' contractual obligations included or referred to in their subsidiary agreements entered into with the AE.

# Programmatic Project Eligibility Criteria

Each of the Projects which will be included in the Program – under the present Funding Proposal and any subsequent phases - will have to meet the following eligibility criteria that are aligned with and organized along the GCF Investment Criteria, prior to being approved by the Accredited Entity's approval body and being able to access the Facility financing:

- **Impact potential:**
  - The project will directly support:
    * (i) the financing and the integration of solar and wind energy representing a minimum of 15 percent of future total installed capacity by 2025 in the given country (grid or), 
    * (ii) the financing and the integration of a minimum of 60 percent of future solar and wind installed capacity by 2025 in the given country (or grid), or
    * (iii) the financing of a renewable energy project that is not requesting financing for Sub-component 2.1 and 2.2.
  
  - The project will support the country in its effort to leverage private sector investment and efficient use of public resources, taking into account on the current security and macro-fiscal constraints that the country may have. However, even in Fragile, Conflicted-affected Situations (FCS) or other countries with high levels of fragility, the project will support private sector leveraging to reduce the impact of large-scale infrastructure financing onto the government's budget.

  - The project will directly finance sources and will instead support VRE deployment by financing:
    * i) investment for grid modernization and upgrade to improve grid’s flexibility and reliability (e.g., new substations, expansion of existing substations, STATCOM, capacitor banks, substation upgrades with transformer, battery storage, transmission lines/upgrade/crossed voltage, and new transmission lines), limit curtailment or grid instability using innovative solutions such as smart grids, microgrids, energy storage, and other technical solutions which may be developed after the date of this FP, whose reliability and efficiency will be assessed by the AE at the time of its internal approval of the project) and adapt to climate change related shocks (techniques such as lines and substation following climate change resilience guidelines)
    * ii) investment in VRE generation capacity (e.g., solar PV, wind turbines, etc.) and connecting the park to the grid, fencing, security system and all infrastructure needed to complete the park (e.g. inverters, transformers, switchgear, cables, substations, etc.), including home systems and mini-grids or any climate friendly technologies)
  - If the project is implemented under Sub-component 3.1 or Sub-component 3.2, it will cover risks related to long-term energy supply security and will be implemented in a competitive manner, and/or as RE investments in utility-scale or mini-grids.

- **Paradigm shift potential:**

## B

The project will focus on increasing RE deployment in the country (grid-connecting and/or off-grid) in a transformative manner – from different angles such as legal, procurement, technical, economic and social development etc., depending on the starting point of the country.

The project will develop a multi-year action plan to be financed under the project to support the long-term impact of the change promoted under the project:

- **Sustainable development potential:**
  - The project will, to the extent possible, support the development of an industrial integration plan to support the deployment of RE technologies.
  - The project will, to the extent possible, support the development of a local development action plan to increase the resilience and enhance the livelihoods of the local communities.

- **Country ownership:**
  - The relevant Host Country will demonstrate a strong willingness to develop a comprehensive and streamlined framework to deploy and integrate RE and leverage in a sustainable manner private investment.
  - The VRE projects and revised policy supported by the project will be aligned with the NDC/Net-emission development plans as reflected in the no-objection letter issued by the Host Country.

- **Recipients' needs:**
  - The Host Country will be vulnerable to climate change and in need to fundamentally adapt its energy systems by harnessing its domestic renewable resources to mitigate climate change.

## Efficiency and effectiveness

The project will be designed to substantially reduce GHG emissions in the given country compared to BAU in the energy sector of the Host Country(ies) and support the achievement of the Host Country(ies)/NDC:

- The project’s expected ratio of total USD per carbon dioxide invested* is maximum 45, and per GCF USD invested – the GCF Proceeds – is maximum 10 for each project excluding projects that are not requiring financing for Sub-component 2.1 and 2.2, and full grant projects.
- The GSR will be calculated as the ratio of total investment cost divided by expected lifetime emissions reductions. The GSR should be no more than 15 percent from the initial budget allocated per Host Country as indicated in Table below:
- The project will be designed to achieve a minimum co-financing ratio GCF to co-financing of 3 (one to two co-financing) in each country.
- The project will have been prepared in compliance with the Accredited Entity’s applicable policies and procedures, and relevant provisions of the World Bank’s PA.
- The project will be approved by the World Bank within 3 years after the Facility’s GCF Board approval.

The proposed project will contribute to closing gender gaps in the energy subsector by (i) identifying gaps relevant to the four pillars of the World Bank Group (WBG) Gender Strategy, and (ii) aiming to address these gaps through specific actions supported by the project.

The projects presented in this Facility are aligned with the eligibility criteria proposed, as illustrated in the feasibility study. The implementing agency will work with each Executing Entity covered under this Facility to finalize the scope and co-financing of these projects, taking into account (i) the national budgetary constraints as they may evolve; (ii) the national development strategy; and (iii) the national dialogue involving all relevant stakeholders.

The project team will closely coordinate with the operational teams to ensure that the preparatory work carried out on the project is adequate for the projects to meet all the eligibility criteria as agreed under the Facility with the GCF.


### Identified Project's Scope and Financing

Subject to IIBRD/IDB/ESMAP corporate approval in accordance with due course, the projects currently considered for inclusion in the Program are expected to be financed through GCF financing instruments from IIBRD in the form of loans and IDA and ESMAP in the form of non-reimbursable grants. Co-financing does not include any other sources of financing for separate (even if related) projects, nor does it include co-financing for the same project under a different Proposal or private investments that are only leveraged. CTF financing may be sought for Mali however, this financing is not yet approved. Given the limited CTF resources expected to be available, the commitment of these resources will depend on the availability of IIBRD/IDB/ESMAP resources and the CTF portion is currently included in the Wb financing described in the present Funding Proposal and therefore there is no financing gap in what is presented.

If the CTF resources are not approved by the World Bank, CTF resources will replace IIBRD/ESMAP resources. In the event that the CTF resources are not approved for Mali, the project will be submitted to the CTF Trust Fund Committee for approval post the GCF Board targeted for the SRM Facility (B28). Meanwhile, these projects are included in the CTF pipeline and the World Bank is expected to operationalize the CTF facility by Q4 2019.

The World bank targets to submit a request for CTF financing for the Mali project upon opening of the new CTF window supporting VRE integration. However, as this window is expected to be operational by late 2019, it will not impact the current timeline.

## B.4 Implementation arrangements (max. 1500 words, approximately 3 pages plus diagrams)

The following section describes the overall Facility management, the management process to ensure the most transformational projects are selected, and to deploy the legal arrangements between GCF, World Bank and the countries.

### 1. SRMI Facility as a GCF Program

The SRMI Facility is a program to support seven projects that currently propose to support seven projects in Indonesia, India, and Vietnam. All projects which will ultimately be supported by the SRMI Facility will have the common purpose to scale-up RE in a systematic and integrated manner. This Programme aims to enhance coordination and strategic alignment of the projects in a manner that maximizes their impact on scaling up RE. The SRMI Facility is being developed as a Programme to ensure enough flexibility to include countries under subsequent phases, such as Indonesia and Vietnam.

This Funding Proposal includes indicative information for each of the currently proposed seven projects. Under the programmatic approach, the GCF would rely on the AE's due diligence on the proposed projects included in the Program. Subject to the outcome of such due diligence, the World Bank will approve funding for such projects that meet the eligibility criteria as presented in Section B3.

The EE are responsible for ensuring the implementation of the projects and the fulfillment of their deliverables according to the AE. The AE will provide implementation support to the EE to the extent permitted under its policies.

### 2. Implementation Arrangements

As part of its role as GCF AE and co-financer of the projects to be supported under the Facility, the World Bank will assess and confirm the eligibility of each such Project to be included in the Facility and benefit from the Bank's and GCF Financing. In order to do so, for each project proposed to be included in the Facility, the Bank will conduct a due diligence exercise that will ensure alignment with the AE's assessment of the Facility's overall objectives and ensure that the projects selected meet all the eligibility criteria (and their limitations) as presented in Section B3.


---
**Page 30**
---

Following the GCF Board approval, the World Bank and the GCF will, based on the Accreditation Master Agreement (AMA), enter into one Funded Assistance Agreement (FAA) for the provision of the funds under all GCF approved projects in the Facility. The World Bank will be responsible for the management and administration of GCF resources and will carry out such management and administration in accordance with its policies, procedures and practices, and the relevant provisions of the FAA and AMA. The World Bank will ensure that the use of GCF funds is consistent with the objectives of the Fund, including but not limited to: countering financing of terrorism, fraud, financial sanctions, embargoes and anti-money laundering.

The World Bank will inform the GCF Secretariat about projects selected in accordance with the Eligibility Criteria as part of the periodic reporting outlined in Section E7. During the implementation of the Facility, the World Bank will ensure that all projects are subject to adequate due diligence, quality assurance in accordance with its policies, procedures and any specific requirements in the AMA and FAA.

The World Bank will use its operation systems and established processes to review individual project proposals that would meet the eligibility criteria as presented in the present Funding Proposal and approve additional projects for funding. The World Bank will also ensure adequate due diligence on SRFs and other projects, as well as the learning across projects. Following approval of Projects, the World Bank will sign Subsidiary Agreements with the EEs and other agreements as applicable with any leadgrant PIES and guarantees as required by the Facility. The World Bank will provide technical assistance to the GCF Secretariat on its website where EEs can use its GCF finance for investments in line with the provisions of the FAA.

There is no capacity assessment of the countries presently in the Facility, as those will be conducted during the appraisal stage of each project. When the projects go through the appraisal stage, some of the capacity assessments may be found to be inadequate and this information will be disclosed accordingly. As part of the World Bank's due diligence in accordance with its own policies and procedures, WB ensures that adequate fiduciary (procurement and financial management (FM), safeguards, legal and M&I) arrangements are put in place by the EEs to ensure that they have sufficient capacity to manage their capacity. (i) they provide recommendations in the event where the capacity is not sufficient and (ii) an effective project management unit (PMU) with the core experts is usually part of the legal covenants for EEs to meet.

# Overview of the indicative mechanism contemplated for the reimbursable grants, subject to future due diligence, market sounding, and World Bank management approval:

## a. Eligible sub-projects: RE private sector projects selected by the Executing Entity, with the technical assistance of the World Bank to ensure that they meet both the eligibility criteria as well as the Bank's mandated environmental and social standards for all eligible sub-projects, as stated in the GCF financing agreement between the Accredited Entity and the Executing Entity.

## b. Executing Entity (the): A Host Country, expected to be the Democratic Republic of Congo, or a state-level entity acting on behalf of the DRC government.

## c. Beneficiaries: private sector developers preparing a public-private partnership for the development of renewable energy projects in the off-grid space.

## d. Covered Risk: mitigation of the critical risks related to private capital investment in the off-grid space, mainly demand-off-take risk affecting the investors for the first few years of operation and consequently the viability of the project (i.e., financing available to the investors [no support viability during the initial years of the sub-projects]).

## e. Financial structure: Disbursement to the project company established by the private developers to implement the renewable energy projects in the off-grid space, which will be made at a certain point of the operation period during which demand/revenues are ramping up and may not be sufficient to reach viability of the mini grid projected in the pre-agreed business plan.

Specifically, disbursement will be made after a certain period (longer than when the date when the demand/revenues in the pre-agreed business plan are sufficient to achieve viability as reflected in the pre-agreed business plan).

- Triggering events to draw down expected to be set by reference to the verified level of the project company revenues below the agreed viability level in the audited business plan during initial (and related) years.

- Triggering event for reimbursement expected to be set by reference to the verified level of the project company revenues above the pre-agreed viability threshold.

### Overview of the indicative mechanism contemplated for the guarantees:
- a. Eligible projects: RE private sector projects competitively selected by the Host Country or a state-owned company mandated by the Host Country, with the technical assistance of the World Bank.
- b. Executing Entity(ies): private sector RE project company
  - Beneficiary: private sector RE project company (in case of the direct payment guarantee) or commercial international or local credit issuing banks providing payment security to such beneficiary in case of a letter-of-credit payment guarantee, or commercial international or local lender to the private sector RE project company.
- c. Covered Risk: Two of the three structures (the letter-of-credit payment guarantee and direct payment guarantee) are designed to provide support for commercial lenders (against public-sector risks related to short-term mobilization of working capital), while the third structure (direct lending) could provide appropriate support to commercial lenders (against public-sector risks related to long-term mobilization of private capital) to private investors that is, Executing Entity RE project company.
- d. Financial Terms: The conditions of guarantee would reflect World Bank's policy and procedural requirements for guarantees, as well as the financial terms agreed upon by all parties involved in each project. In accordance with World Bank's policies and procedures, guarantees would only be provided to the extent necessary to mobilize private financing for the projects and/or to mitigate risk-related risks of the projects, taking into account country, project, and market circumstances.

projects will follow the WB project cycle, as well as the Bank's operational policies and procedures. For each Project included in the Programme, including those for which a GCF financing would be requested, the World Bank will carry out due diligence and submit an appraisal-stage Project Appraisal Document (PAD) for quick approval by the Board. If the PAD is approved, and if the project meets all the relevant World Bank requirements, the project moves to negotiations and World Bank Board approval stage, which cover the GCF financing and an WB or WB-administered co-financing. This unit consists of a technical expert, procurement specialist, financial management specialist, environmental specialist and social/gender specialist, and a lawyer. Each project will have its own monitoring and evaluation (M&E) and reporting process, and the EE will be accountable for its implementation.
