## B.5. Justification for GCF funding request (max. 1000 words, approximately 2 pages)

GCF funding is critical to address the current market conditions applied and to ensure that countries continue on their carbon-dioxide emission path. Today, the deployment of solar and wind is constrained due to: (i) lack of existing government strategy to adapt the current energy sector to the new disruptive technologies; (ii) weak grids that require upfront investments by public parties; (iii) government financing constraints; (iv) high costs of grid expansion; (v) low levels of private investment in renewable and domestic production/import of fossil fuel. VRE deployment is also more expensive when accounting the integration cost and when there is no organized competitive selection of IPPs. VRE deployment is not always compatible with existing transmission infrastructure, which requires significant investments to be made by the grid operator. The country has to choose between promoting least-cost coal production versus slightly more expensive and more complex to integrate VRE as the primary source.

The situation is aggravated by the Covid-19 crisis which is impacting the RE deployment today. It is more difficult for governments to spare public financing for critical public investments needed to unlock private investments as public financing is prioritized to tackle critical challenges hindering health and jobs. In addition, the pandemic has led to a significant drop in electricity demand, which is expected to remain low until 2021 due to reduced industrial activity and lower residential consumption. This reduced demand affects their revenues, and therefore, private investors are perceiving them as riskier, increasing the premium on their cost of capital which in turn would increase the electricity price.

To face these challenges, the SMMI Facility is providing an integrated risk mitigation framework to (i) reduce the cost of privately-owned generation (lowering the risk premium embedded in the tariff making)


---
**Page 36**
---

The electronic grid is more affordable,
(ii) provide concessional funding leveraging climate finance to unlock critical public investments needed to enable private investments making them more affordable for the countries, and (iii) maximize socio-economic benefits fostering jobs creation, skills development and women’s empowerment.

Catalytic GCF concessional financing will be critical as (i) some countries among the poorest in the world are undergoing dramatic tensions on their budgets due to Covid-19; (ii) all will turn towards the electrification of energy with a significant share of renewable energy sources; and (iii) with the investment environment improving, concessionality is the last mile to raising private investors’ interest. Under the current GCF approach, concessional financing is not available for projects that are already bankable. However,
the financing and grant technical assistance to support developing countries in their energy transition are more critical than ever. Access to concessional public climate finance and DFID financing would enable solar and wind projects to be developed at a lower cost compared with the private sector. This is especially true as
governments grapple with the critical task of dealing with the very real impacts of Covid-19 on the health and welfare of their citizens, while also ensuring that they are not left behind in the transition towards renewable energy generation unless there is strong parallel support for renewable energy development. Therefore, countries need to be supported financially and technically to revise their current plans and implement the revised ones to ensure that they can access concessional financing from the GCF and DFID to avoid being priced out of competing with the billions of USD spent on fossil fuel subsidies. In addition, by using concessionary financing to reduce perceived risks by private sector investors, countries would be able to unlock at scale privately financed renewable energy projects in developing countries, which are currently not accessible for most
of the countries.
The GCF, thanks to its mandate and scale, will enable a scale-up of the SRM approach that will have real impact. In addition, supporting the scale-up of RE through a systematical integrated and open approach would help to ensure that the transition is in line with the Paris Agreement and sustainable development pathways.

The climate concessional financing under the Initiative is also essential to support innovative technologies with a significant mitigation potential that are not yet commercially viable such as battery storage. GCF’s risk appetite will be critical to provide innovative support to these countries. The industry supporting these paradigms- shifting disruptive technologies at its a nascent stage and will also suffer from the lack of experience in deploying them at scale. In this context, concessional financing for investments in dispatch and system operation – including battery storage, VRE generation is likely to be required. This is especially true as countries are not yet prepared to take on the financial risk associated with the risk arising from lack of experience in deploying these technologies at scale in the context of developing countries; private sector investors will not be willing to invest in these innovative technologies without concessionality.

Private investments in the off-grid RE space requires adapted risk mitigation instruments that can be developed with climate financing that is more flexible than DFI guarantees. A risk mitigation mechanism, as described in section 3.1., that covers a wider range of risks and provides flexibility will be an important catalyst in helping attract much needed private sector investment and scale up mini grids and SHS.
This demand risk is not covered by traditional DFI risk mitigation mechanisms like MIGA insurance or WB guarantees. The SRM Facility can provide this type of flexible risk mitigation mechanism.

With the exception of countries which receive full grants from the World Bank for instance due to their level of GDP per capita or post-conflict status (such as CAR in the current list of projects proposed to be supported by the SRM Facility), the allocation between grants and loans is done between technical assistance/capacity building and infrastructure. GCF is providing grants in CAR


---
**Page 37**
---

because it is an FCS country and is recovering from the recent major domestic political and security crisis in 2013. The ensuing insurging led to massive forced displacement, the collapse of state institutions, and devastation of the country’s economy. The country is in dire need of IDA grant to recover its infrastructure and build back better. It is also one of the top 5 countries with the highest number of people in need of assistance out of 188 countries on the United Nations Human Development Index. The other seven countries are split between three IDA and three IBRD. As per the SRMI methodology, the activities promoted under the technical assistance component of the SRMI will be used to support the identification of catalytic climate finance and affordable generation mix as well as developing an enabling environment to private sector mobilization. Therefore, without targeted grant technical assistance, these studies are usually not being completed which leads to far from optimal decision with regards to new generation or transmission investments. The GCF has a critical role in providing this targeted grant technical assistance where the resources needed to ensure that the most fragile countries are able to reduce their debt weight and the cost of the interest.

Private investments are critical to enable the sustainable upscaling of private investments. However, governments are often stretched in their budget due to overwhelming social sector priorities. In this context, catalytic climate financing will help support more investments in RE. The approach promoted under the SRMI is to provide targeted grant technical assistance for the identification of affordable generation mix limited to critical public investment required for a higher penetration of renewables such as VRE integration investment, and solar parks infrastructure. However, governments need to continuously look at the budget allocation for these projects and ensure that they are not being diverted towards other sectors which were exacerbated by the Covid-19 crisis. The possibility to access climate financing for those critical infrastructure investments is also a key driver of private sector participation in the energy sector as well as in the health or health sectors. The level of GCF contribution in the financing of the currently proposed projects has been determined based on discussions with countries and their possibility to increase their financing for their own account.

Another aspect of the SRMI Facility that will support an increased public investment allocation towards critical investments is the concessionality of GCF compared to IBRD and to a certain extent IDA as well. Under the SRMI Framework, GCF high concessional terms for vulnerable countries set at 0% interest rate and 50% grant component (with a maximum term limit of 20 years) will be used by the IBRD countries to make the investment towards low-carbon infrastructure fostering low-emission development.

The GCF high concessional terms will enable the investments to be viable for both IDA and IBRD countries – and in turn the concessionality will fully benefit the electricity households of these countries. The GCF high concessional loan terms with a maturity period of 40 years and 10 years grace period (with a maximum term limit of 25 years) will be used by IDA countries to make investments at an interest rate of 0.50 percent commitment fee) will result in an increase of 2 percent in project internal rate of return (IRR).

Given the abovementioned rationales, the GCF financing will be critical from the additonality perspective to unlock the sustainable programs presented by the seven countries included in this Framework. The SRMI methodology provides a unique opportunity for these countries to access more affordable, (i) identifying risk-dragging measures meeting the needs of the private sector through the availability of this concessional financing (e.g. mitigating the development risk for the private sector by providing guarantees or other forms of insurance), (ii) developing innovative and/or enhanced public-private partnerships (PPPs) that are more attractive to the private sector, (iii) identifying innovative risk mitigation approaches that are complex to provide by DFIs but that are within the mandate of the GCF and (iv) unlocking sustainable and bankable projects under SRMI at scale.


---
**Page 38**
---

With the support of the GCF financing, the integrated risk mitigation framework promoted under the SRMI Facility is expected to play a key role on (i) a short-term basis by contributing to a green stimulus package for recovery and (ii) a medium-to-long-term basis by ensuring a clean development path, mitigating significantly the GHG emissions trajectory.

### B.6 Exit strategy and sustainability max. 500 words approximately 1 page)

Sustainability is at the heart of SRMI mainstreamed in a cross-cutting integrated approach. Supporting countries to design and implement a sustainable low-emission development pathway, from a multi-dimensional perspective, fostering a transformational change with projects replicable at the level of the countries themselves.

a. From an economic perspective, ensuring through comprehensive planning that sound RE targets are defined based on least cost planning, factoring in integration of VRE and grid resilience, and promoting a sector-coupling approach to the extent possible.

b. From a financial perspective, ensuring that SRMI provides co-financing or sector financing by supporting the design and implementation of bankable projects that could be financed by the private sector.

c. From a strategic perspective, ensuring upfront that the RE program designed is aligned with the countries priorities and strategic considerations (including in terms of risk allocation and roles and responsibilities) to ensure its sustainability over time.

d. From an environmental perspective, financing countries’ NDCs on an economically viable manner while ensuring their implementation.

e. From institutional and regulatory perspective, strengthening the countries' framework and developing the risk mitigation strategy by financing the transaction advisors for the first round of projects under the RE program defined allowing a “learning by doing” with hands-on experience to give to the seven or more countries that will be implementing the projects, and fully integrate the changes they are facing in one environment and into their long-term plans.

f. From a socio-economic perspective, maximizing the positive impacts on the local industries, (positing the local players on the solar/wind value chain in a competitive manner to the extent possible), ensuring that the RE projects have a positive impact on the social inclusion of women and enhancing their livelihoods).

g. From a gender perspective, ensuring that men and women benefit from the RE projects launched with an emphasis on how to promote female employment throughout the RE project cycle as otherwise men may benefit disproportionately from new employment opportunities across the RE value chain.

h. From a financial perspective, SRMI’s core investments in infrastructure for the grid and with regards to electrification are long-term investments that have long-term potential impact and will be key to the economic development of countries. The expected increase in the share of renewable energy (RE) integration is expected to enable a shift on average from 7 percent penetration of VRE to over 35 percent by 2035 and electrification investments are core to the development of a population.

Therefore, the approach used by the SRMI Facility is supporting countries in developing a multi-dimensional sustainable RE program and operationalizing the first round of bankable projects. By laying strong foundations and implementing an adequate capacity building program key public stakeholders


---
**Page 39**
---

will be able to capitalize on the work done, the track-record built, and the skills developed for their next round of RE projects.

The SBM Facility is expected to act as a catalyst to unlock these sustainable RE programs in the countries and pave the way to operationalize them through bankable projects privately financed. With a strong framework in place, a successful track-record and a sustainable ecosystem implemented, countries would be in a position to replicate these projects and attract private investment in optimized conditions.

## Promoting a sustainable environment for private investments as part of the energy sector is critical to the SRMI approach.

Capacity building is key from that perspective to ensure that local stakeholders are effectively empowered in relation to the solar/wind value chain to the extent possible. A multi-dimensional capacity-building framework was considered under SRMI for that purpose, covering from the design of the RE strategy to their implementation.

To increase the effectiveness of capacity building, the approach promotes a strategic analysis upfront to identify the targeted roles and responsibilities of the key stakeholders and assess the local infrastructure and resources available to them. This will allow the SRMI Facility to develop an action plan to develop the targeted skills of the key stakeholders and improve their chances to benefit from their own RE projects. For example, by defining a cross-cutting country program to position the local players in the market, the SRMI Facility is able to provide a comprehensive package that includes a mix of knowledge needed as well as from the hands-on experience thanks to adequate arrangements reflected in the tender documents. This plan will benefit from the support of transaction advisors financed under SRMI to accompany the countries in this pathway through learning by doing approach for the pre-identified stakeholders.

In addition, as integration of VRE is a key aspect, SRMI is also promoting a transfer of knowledge in favor of key local stakeholders in relation to storage and more particularly with respect to battery storage. SRMI is leveraging the Energy Storage Partnership (ESP) launched by the World Bank in cooperation with the Global Environment Facility (GEF). The ESP is yet another initiative aimed at enabling higher penetration of VRE in the grid. The SRMI Facility is directly contributing to the development of the battery storage technology by enabling the countries to implement it, building a track-record as well as crucial knowledge.

**Page 43**
---

D. EXPECTED PERFORMANCE AGAINST INVESTMENT CRITERIA

This section refers to the performance of the project/programme against the investment criteria as set out in the GCF’s Initial Investment Framework.

### D.1 Impact potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The seven proposed projects are expected to contribute to the shift to low-emission sustainable development by:

- Directly supporting the development of 2.5 GW of RE.
    - (i) 1.6 GW of solar PV.
    - (ii) 400 MW of CSP, and
    - (iii) 300 MW of onshore wind. Of this 2.1 GW are expected to be built during the lifetime of the projects.
- Indirectly supporting the development of 7.5 GW of RE through Sub-Component 1.3 financing the solar and wind park analysis and Sub-Component 2.1 financing the public infrastructure of solar and wind park infrastructure and Sub-Component 1.2 for the hydroelectric power plants. This indirect support is expected to lead to the development of another 1.9 GW of RE.
- The integration of other projects is enabled thanks to the VRE integration analysis conducted under Sub-Component 1.1 and Sub-Component 2.2 under which core and infrastructure are being financed.

#### Directly reduce GHG emissions by 97.93 metric tons of CO₂e, more precisely 58.96% reduction for the 2.1 GW projects and 40.4% reduction for the 0.4 GW projects. This corresponds to 1.1 million tons of CO₂ for 0.4 GW of RE projects that were prepared under the projects but not built during the lifetime of the projects.

The GHG emission reduction is based on RE projects being built:

- Unlock USD 3.3 billion of private investments, including USD 3 billion for the 2.1 GW projects during the lifetime of the project and USD 0.4 billion for the 0.4 GW projects outside of the lifetime of the projects.
- The private investment is leveraged thanks to Component 1.2 transaction advisory and the general environment and bankable support provided to them through the general SRMI support of technical assistance, capacity building, and project preparation under Sub-Component 3.1 for infrastructure under construction or in operation and guarantees and risk mitigation instruments under Component 3.
- Provide access to clean low-emission electricity to 4.2 million people thanks to the de-siloing of RE projects through financing public investments in clean energy mini-grids and connection to the grid that would have been made greener thanks to Sub-Components 1.2 and 2, and Sub-Component 3.1 that will enable unlocking private investments for green mini-grids.
- Strengthen institutional and regulatory systems for planning and development.

#### Reduce locking-in long-lived, climate-vulnerable infrastructure by increasing resilience of grid infrastructure.

### D.2. Paradigm shift potential (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)
By tackling in a coordinated manner planning, procurement, development and curtailment risks to attract affordable private investments, SRMI aims at supporting countries in their shift to low-emission development pathways. Most countries have reached a point where most of their "easy" RE projects are already implemented or under construction. As the remaining RE projects become more challenging, the majority of the electricity is produced from RE sources, governments will need to put in place sustainable generation plans, upgrade their grids and develop the right legal and procurement framework to leverage private investments. The SRMI Facility aims at supporting countries in this transition by providing a funding framework and will require strong governments buy-in and ownership. The SRMI Facility aims at accompanying governments in their path to low-emission development while ensuring it does not become an economic burden.

### Building on a sound methodology**, SRMI aims at supporting countries transform their strategy to low-emission development allowing them to harness their domestic resources at scale.
- **The first step** is to a sound deployment of the setting of sustainable long-term targets for RE based on country-specific needs and opportunities. The SRMI Facility will support countries in defining these targets which will be the basis for a realistic but also ambitious plan to deploy RE in the given country. Through the SRMI Facility, the World Bank is supporting the seven countries to revise their generation plans and to identify the most appropriate technologies to meet their energy needs while upgrading their grids ensuring its reliability and resilience. These targets will then need to be integrated into their NDC revisions ensuring they are implementable.

### After the targets are set
- The SRMI Facility is supporting the seven countries to develop a long-term strategy on how to finance those projects. The strategy looks at who and how to finance the deployment of RE and the associated infrastructure. It also identifies all the legal changes that need to be implemented to attract private investments and how the domestic lending sector can be leveraged to finance the bulk of the total project cost while ensuring a stable power supply.


---
**Page 45**
---

The main pillar to the paradigm shift is capacity building. As part of SRM1, it is critical to increase capacity within the government on all fronts so that afterwards they will be able to conduct analysis without technical assistance and implement their own projects in a sustainable manner in the sector on the ground.

After supporting the development of the country’s long-term targets, its strategy to implement them and increase capacity, the SRM Facility is financing the implementation its strategy. It is critical to combine these three elements: the country’s long-term targets, its strategy to implement them, and a combined approach, the countries may not be able to finance the targets or have the right target in the first place.

As a consequence, countries will be able to operationalize their NDCs in a sustainable and timely manner in spite of the effect of the Covid-19 crisis, reducing significantly their GHG emissions trajectory by shifting to a lasting low-emission development pathway. Built on strong foundations through the SRM approach, this is a multi-dimensional sustainability perspective at its heart. This low-emission development pathway will be replicable, fast-tracking the road towards the Sustainable Development Goals for these countries.

The seven countries are representative of different types of markets/energy sector, with low to high electrification rates and varying levels of energy access. The lessons learned from the SRM approach as learnt from those seven countries will enable to replicate the SRM approach across countries and enable to unlock sustainable RE markets for private investments. SRM is currently being implemented considered for implementation in 10 additional countries in Sub-Saharan Africa and Asia which are part of this first phase.

Tailored support for each country will be provided under the SRM approach. The status of RE development varies from different one from country to another and will be further assessed at Project’s level to leverage the existing related activities. The technical assistance deployed under the SRM Facility will be tailored for each country needs and will, to the extent possible, build on the current RE activities deployed, in order to avoid duplication and ensure a coherent approach.

Sound planning associated with adequate grid investments to enable a higher generation of renewable energy and resilient grid will lead to the development of SUSTAINABLE RE targets. It will avoid the lock-in of the countries in long-term high-emission development pathways. Harnessing domestic resources such as solar or wind for generation will also reduce the need for fuel imports and the potential environmental impacts associated with them, while contributing to the achievement of SDG 7 Affordable and Clean Energy.

Sustainability is at the heart of the multi-dimensional approach of SRM1. The SRM1 approach aims at developing a lasting low-emission targeted countries if a sustainable economy ensuring a lasting and replicable low-emission development pathway. It will be particularly relevant for the achievement of SDG 7 Affordable and Clean Energy.

The seven countries are: Benin, Ethiopia, Ghana, Kenya, Mozambique, Namibia, and Uganda

Their aim is to increase their energy independence while reducing the price volatility (as electricity tariffs are set for a long-term period, around 25 years).

The operationalization of these RE targets through bankable projects with a fair risk allocation, building on a strengthened institutional and regulatory framework with adequate risk mitigation arrangements, will be achieved by ensuring that the private sector is able to make investments at scale, ensuring the availability of the commercial financing and the appetite of the private sector to take these projects forward. The project pipeline will include 100% renewable energy projects which are expected to create jobs in sectors such as health/education, particular in the context of COVID-19.

The strategic analysis upfront to define an action plan to maximize the socio-economic benefits of the RE program (embedded in the SMID methodology), will be mainstreamed during the operationalization phase. The aim is to ensure that the RE projects are integrated into a broader value chain in a competitive manner to the extent possible, maximizing jobs creation, skills development and transfer of knowledge. It will also support local community development in the areas of implementation of the projects.

The project pipeline will include 100% renewable energy projects which are expected to create jobs in sectors such as health/education, particular in the context of COVID-19.

Capacity building will be mainstreamed throughout the RE projects cycle, strengthening the countries’ framework and developing the skills needed (including through a learning by doing approach with the support of transaction advisors) to give to the seven countries the ability to replicate efficiently the projects. Building on the strong foundations of the program, key public stakeholders will be able to capitalize on the work done, the stakeholder built, and the skills developed for their next round of RE projects.

The GDP growth forecast for 2020 will be reviewed with the current Covid-19 pandemic crisis. The countries also have very different country investment ratings, with only Botswana considered as Investment grade.

The SRMI facility is designed to provide a flexible and innovative approach, without impeding economic development or putting at risk their grid and therefore, their industrial development.

The countries considered will need strong support to strengthen their institutions and implement reforms to ensure that they can integrate renewable grids into the national power system with support to work on their priorities on their technical and regulatory constraints and as a second step managing the implementation of their new strategy, such as with regards to managing battery storage into their grid, procuring IPPs and adapting their grid to electric mobility development.

## D

### b. CAR: The annual emissions of the Central African Republic, estimated at 116,285.49 kt eqCO₂ in 2010, or 26 tons eqCO₂ per person, represent less than 0.002 percent of global emissions. Despite its low rate of economic growth and limited access to energy services, the CAR is committed to a path of collective but differentiated responsibility and, in accordance with Decision 1/CP.19, is taking the present ambitious measures to respond to this challenge without impeding its economic, social and environmental development. The country’s emissions are projected to increase by an average rate of 25 percent, respectively, in the 2030 and 2050 horizons in comparison to its reference BAU emission scenario. This is due to the country’s energy potential. With international support, it will emit around 33,076 kt eqCO₂ by 2050 than the current reference scenario.

### c. DRC: As one of the most fragile states and poorest countries in the world, DRC faces a number of challenges which it seeks to address through its climate targets and focus on the SDGs. The contribution of the forestry sector is estimated at 13.7 percent of total emissions (2010). GHG intensity relative to GDP is also low. DRC has committed to reduce its GHG emissions by 17 percent by 2030, focusing specifically on three sectors – energy, agriculture and land use, land use change, and forestry (LULUCF) - which collectively produce nearly 98 percent of its GHG emissions.

### d. Kenya: Kenya’s planning process on mitigation and adaptation hinges on the National Climate Change Action Plan (NCCAP) and the National Adaptation Plan (NAP). The two will be reviewed every five years to ensure that they are in line with national development plans. Mitigation actions are further amplified in the NAP. Mitigation and adaptation actions are implemented across the various sectors at both the national and county government levels. The Ministry of Environment and Forestry is responsible for coordinating climate change activities through the National Climate Secretariat (NCS). The planning process follows the Climate Change Act (2016) that introduced several mechanisms to enhance coordination of climate change adaptation and mitigation.

These include the establishment:

- A high-level National Climate Change Council chaired by the President. The Council shall provide overall direction on national climate change policy, including the mainstreaming of climate change functions by the national and county governments, and approve and oversee the implementation of the NCCAP.
- A County Climate Change Coordinating Committee at each county government on national climate change plans and actions to deliver operational coordination; and provide technical assistance on climate change actors and responses to County governments.

### g. Uzbekistan: In the long-term perspective up to 2030, Uzbekistan intends to strengthen measures and actions to struggle against climate change, in order to achieve the mitigation objectives (i) to decrease carbon emissions of energy-related gases per unit GDP by 10 percent by 2030 from level of 2010. Achievement of the INDC long-term objective requires a number of measures at national organizations and financial institutions, ensuring access to the advanced energy saving and environmental technologies. The Uzbekistan government has already adopted and is implementing legally binding programs and acts aimed at ensuring by 2020 the implementation of measures for decrease in energy consumption, introduction of energy-saving technologies in various sectors of the economy, as well as improvement of energy efficiency. Decree No. 1437 of the President of the Republic of Uzbekistan: "On Measures for Further Development of Alternative Energy Sources"; Decree No. 2058 of the President of the Republic of Uzbekistan: "On Measures for Supporting Structural Reformation, Modernization and Diversification of Production for 2015-2019"; Resolution of the President of the Republic of Uzbekistan: "On Program of Measures for Reduction in Energy Consumption and Introduction of Energy-Saving Technologies in Various Sectors of Economy"; Decree No. 3487 of the President of the Republic of Uzbekistan: "On Action Program of Environmental Protection of the Republic of Uzbekistan; Resolution of the Cabinet of Ministers of the Republic of Uzbekistan: "On Integrated Program of Actions for Mitigation of the Adverse Effects of Climate Change in the Aral Sea and South-Western Regions of the Republic Region for 2015-2018"; Program for Further Development of Agricultural Production, Program for Further Irrigated Lands Improvement and Rational Use of Water Resources.

## D.6 Efficiency and effectiveness (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

Since this is a Programme-based facility, the financial sustainability will vary from project to project. The programme will be evaluated in terms of cost-effectiveness, co-financing and leveraging, financial viability, and application of best practices.

- Cost-effectiveness and efficiency


---
**Page 50**
---

The GCF plays a catalytic role to enable VRE at scale through the comprehensive and integrated risk mitigation framework proposed under SRM by: (i) financing the critical public investments needed in an affordable manner and such as reducing the burden for the countries in particular in the context of the Covid-19 pandemic; (ii) providing concessional financing to support the development of renewable energy assets of the private sector through the availability of this concessional financing (e.g. solar/wind park infrastructure to mitigate the development risk); (iii) adapting to the specific needs of countries that need innovative risk mitigation solutions in order to unlock their potential for VRE at scale; (iv) providing a financial mechanism to unlock large-scale mini-grid business models); and (v) unlocking sustainable and bankable projects under SRM at scale.

As described in SB.6, the concessionality of GCF financing will be critical to ensure that the countries are into impactful projects and avoid a path involving deployment of more coal and gas with limited RE. The cost of the public loans is a crucial parameter in the political decision to implement or not a more sustainable path. The GCF additionally is therefore paramount to a successful result. To change the course of a given potential trajectory, it requires concessional financial support from both its NDCs and all of our ongoing dialogue. Therefore, the SRM Facility is requesting the highest level of concessionality for the seven countries that are part of the Program.

The financial structure is a mix between grants for technical assistance; concessional loans for critical public infrastructure – with the exception of CAR which will get full grants due to its FCS situation and risk mitigation challenges; concessional financing for private sector investments – with the exception of the Program’s objective to support countries in their low-emission pathways: (i) grants for key studies and capacity building, (ii) technical assistance for project preparation and implementation by the government, (iii) concessional financing for public investments will support countries in increasing their penetration of VRE and improve grid reliability and resilience; and (iv) risk mitigation instruments are enabling the private sector to invest in VRE at scale, especially in countries where the counter-factual is cheap coal production such as Botswana and Zimbabwe.

The Facility aims to unlock over 2.1 GW of RE in the short-term and 0.4 GW in the medium-term. The division between short-term and medium-term is mostly due to the plan for demand increase and preparation of parks. The expected GHG emission reduction is 89 million tons of CO2e which represents an average GCF disbursement of $15 per ton.

• Co-financing, leveraged and leveraged long-term investments

The USD 360 million GCF will be complemented by an exposed USD 1.3 billion in World Bank financing which in turn is expected to leverage around USD 3 billion in private investments. The indicative leveraging between public investments (World Bank and GCF financing) and private investments is 1 to 3. The private investments are a result of the projects’ public investments and therefore, are not considered as part of the co-financing and the Funded Activity.

• Financial viability

The GCF concessional financing will be critical to improve the economic and financial rate of return of the projects. Keeping in mind that fuel taxes and subsidies are included in the economic analysis when data is available, the GCF financing increases the financial debt on average by around 2 percent but


---
**Page 51**
---

more important makes possible those projects that would not be accepted by the countries without the highly concessional climate financing support. GCF financing is a game changer for the governments as because it is concessional countries which would otherwise select polluting energy sources are now able to join in programs.

The financial analysis conducted for Botswana, Kenya, Mali, Namibia and Uzbekistan for Component 2.1 and 2.2 activities resulted in financial IRR of 13 percent, 18 percent, 27 percent, 12 percent, and 14 percent respectively. The level of concessionality proposed for each of these projects enables the financial IRRs to be financially viable for the countries.

• **Application of best practices**

To ensure that the best available technologies are considered, the government will hire transaction advisors who will develop the request for proposals to IPPs and ensure that the IPPs only select Tier 1 technologies. The best practice with regards to selection of IPPs will also be implemented to ensure financial sustainability of all the contracts and develop a track-record within the countries in selection of IPPs.

### E.7 Monitoring, reporting and evaluation arrangements

Besides the arrangements laid out in the ABA between the World Bank and the GCF, the implementation of the SRM Facility involves (i) monitoring of program performance indicators (as per Section E5); (ii) periodic progress reports; and (iii) midterm review of implementation progress. It is noted that the terms of reference for the SRM Facility are subject to change as more information becomes available on each project that will be financed, and the targets may be revised during AE's due diligence, project preparation and the mid-term review as necessary. The AE will report to GCF the implementation status at both Facility and country level by aggregating specific country-level information and data.

- Monitoring


---
**Page 63**
---

Specifically, the implementation of each project under the GCF facility will be managed and monitored at project and Facility level by World Bank in-house staff with no cost is applicable.

Project Implementation Units (which would include staff and consultants). The World Bank will conduct staff in its energy team, operation departments, Environment and Social departments and country offices that will conduct due diligence, monitor compliance and performance risks, implementation of the Environmental and Social Framework (ESF) and other relevant policies.

The World Bank will follow its standard monitoring and reporting policies and procedures, which will include semi-annual reports on the implementation status and performance of each project. By the end of each project supported by the SRII Facility, the Bank will conduct jointly with each respective Executive Energy Efficiency (EE) Units will be responsible for overall M&E of the project, including collection of project performance information and reporting on project impacts and achievements.

Reporting of EEs to World Bank: As specified in the subsidiary agreements between the EEs and the World Bank, the EEs are obliged to report on the implementation of the Project and the use of proceeds of the Facility in accordance with the terms and conditions of the subsidiary agreements, including on the environmental and social performance of the project. The achievements and indicators agreed upon by the AE and EE will be reported to the World Bank on an annual basis. For each project supported by the SRII Facility, the respective EE will be responsible for overall M&E of the project, including collection of project performance information and reporting on project impacts and achievements.

Reporting of World Bank to GCF: The World Bank will provide to the GCF (i) annual activity performance reports at the midpoint of implementation period of the Facility through the reporting period, (ii) mid-term evaluation reports at the midpoint of the implementation period of the Facility and (iii) final evaluation reports at the end of the implementation period of the Facility as per the FAA provisions. The APRs will provide information specific to projects supported under the Facility, as relevant.

Evaluation

The evaluation methods for mid-term and final evaluations will include:

a. Key informant interviews with relevant beneficiary staff and clients, as well as other key stakeholders, based on a structured questionnaire.
b. Desktop review of relevant Facility documentation, including Project Implementation Unit reports, based on an agreed list of evidence to be provided by the client.

# RISK ASSESSMENT AND MANAGEMENT

## F. Risk factors and mitigations measures (max. 3 pages)

The main risks identified to the successful implementation of the Facility are linked to Governance (political and governance, macroeconomic and sector strategies and policies), Technical and Operational (technical design of projects, institutional capacity for implementation and sustainability, fiduciary and administrative arrangements, project preparation and duration of Covid-19 pandemic) [with wide impact on macroeconomic environment and possibly project preparation and implementation context in various countries] as well as Security in specific project countries.

## Selected Risk Factor 1

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Governance | Medium | Medium |

### Description

Political and Governance. As DRC, CAR and Mali are facing complex political situations, it may impact their political commitment to the projects and the governance aspect of the implementation of the components:

- **DRC**: is still recovering from a series of conflicts that broke out in the 1990s, creating a protracted economic and social slump. Joseph Kabila served as head of state between 2001 and 2019. President Tshisekedi was sworn into office on January 24, 2019, but his legitimacy has been contested until December 30, 2018 to facilitate an overhaul of the electoral register. Felix Antoine Tshisekedi—the son of Etienne Tshisekedi, the country's longstanding opposition leader—secured 38.5 percent of the vote and was sworn in as president on January 24, 2019. The presidential election that preceded this one was contested, including at the National Episcopal Conference of Congo (CENCO), which had deployed 40,000 security forces.

- **CAR**: elections in 2016 brought an end to three years of political transition and turmoil. Since 2018, CAR has been governed by President Faustin-Archange Touadera and Prime Minister Simon Salomé Djotodia. The next presidential election is scheduled for December 30, 2024, but the date may be pushed back.

- **Mali**: has been experiencing instability and conflict since the military coup of 2012 and the fall of the government by armed groups. The operation of the United Nations Multidimensional Integrated Stabilization Mission in Mali (MINUSMA) dates back to July 2014.


### Macroeconomic

This risk category includes external and domestic macroeconomic risks that may derail the achievement of operations development results during either preparation or implementation. Overall, the seven countries have experienced a mixed picture with some countries experiencing growth while others are negative in Namibia, between 2 and 4 percent in Kenya and Botswana and above 5 percent in DRC, Mali and Uzbekistan. Their inflation levels are aligned with their GDP growth rates with levels in 2018 between 0 to 5 percent for all seven countries except Namibia which is at 9.7 percent. The external environment has been a significant widening of the fiscal and external financing gaps and consequently, GDP growth in some countries.

### Sector Strategies and Policies

It is critical to have in place the right regulatory frameworks (i.e. Kenya are working on new legislation to implement competitive selection of IPPs), (i.e. DRC does not have the full policy


---
**Page 65**
---

place to enable privately-owned mini-grid operations, and (ii) Botswana, Namibia and Malawi are missing clear generation plans with implementation strategies linked to it.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

The World Bank team is working closely with the different Governments to ensure the sustainability and relevance of the projects to the given country. In general, good governance of the proposals will be ensured by the World Bank through its M&E systems. The World Bank will also work with governments to fix financial and other untechnical practices resulting from government failures. These will be enabled thanks to the World Bank implementation and M&E systems.

The projects are expected to strengthen the country's macro-fiscal situations as though the deployment of least-cost renewable energy (LRE) will reduce the cost of imported fossil fuel while reducing energy security risks and improving national carbon footprint.

In addition, the World Bank is working with the seven countries to ensure that a comprehensive regulatory framework is in place when the new investments come in play aiming to ensure the sustainability of the new generation projects.

### Selected Risk Factor 2

| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Technical and operational | Medium | Medium |

**Description**

Technical Design of projects. As solar and wind generation are well known technologies and there are clear investments related to VRE integration, the technical design risk is minimal. In addition, with a clear and fair risk allocation between the private and the public stakeholders, RE projects are least-cost compared to other generation projects.

### Institutional Capacity for Implementation and Sustainability

The institutional capacity of the implementing agencies, implementation arrangements, and M&E arrangements is quite different from one country to the next. Namibia, Botswana, Kenya and Uganda have strong implementing agencies whereas CAR, DR Congo and Malawi do not.

### Risk of RE Projects Not Materializing

Most RE power plants under the Facility will be prepared thanks to public investments. However, the financing for the power plants themselves will be financed by private investors. The private investment is not covered under the Facility.

### Mitigation Measure(s)

To ensure that this risk is mitigated, the World Bank is supporting the proposed seven countries in their selection of the private investors. For the countries with limited capacity, larger capacity building components will be provided with additional external support for project implementation. A transparent and well-organized selection of private investors combined with the reduction in risks from curtailment, land & E&S perspective


---
**Page 66**
---

# GREEN CLIMATE FUND FUNDING PROPOSAL V2.1 PAGE 64 OF 72

Thanks to the park scheme will ensure that private investment is indeed leveraged as a result of the Facility's projects.

Each country has a different fiduciary risk and it is being mitigated by previous experience with each client, and World Bank procurement and financial management requirements.

## Selected Risk Factor 3
| Category | Probability | Impact |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Other | Medium | Medium |

### Description

#### Security: Security risks are key in countries such as Mali, CAR, and DRC.
- Mali's security, which is critical for economic recovery and poverty reduction, remains fragile in the face of continued attacks by armed groups on UN peacekeepers, the Malian army, and civilians, mainly in the north and central regions. In July 2017, Germany, the African Development Bank, the World Bank, France, Italy, Luxembourg, Spain, and the United Kingdom joined the European Union and the G5 Sahel to form the G5 Sahel Force (G5S) with Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Niger. Since then, Denmark, Italy, Luxembourg, the Netherlands, Spain, and the United Kingdom have joined the Alliance.
- On February 6, 2019, the Government of CAR signed an African Union-mediated peace agreement with 14 armed groups in Bangui. The UN Security Council Resolution 2435 (2018) states that the European Union and the Economic Community of Central African States (ECCAS) serve as guarantors of the agreement, while the UN peacekeeping force (MINUSCA) plays a critical support role in the background.
- DRC is fraught with political instability, armed clashes, and human rights violations. Conflict erupted in 2016 in North Kivu province, which has five provinces in the eastern part of the country. It is another instance of fighting between the government and opposition forces. Nationally, over 1 million people were newly displaced in 2017 and 2018, making the DRC the African country with the highest number of internally displaced people —4.5 million.

### Mitigation Measure(s)
The security risk will be monitored, and the projects will rapidly adapt to any change in security levels in the three countries.

G. GCF POLICIES AND STANDARDS

### G.1 Environmental and social risk assessment (max. 750 words, approximately 1.5 pages)

The World Bank has a new Environmental & Social Framework that will be used by all projects. The E&SF is derived in an E&S standards (ESS) that are each assessed based on their relevance depending on the project's concept. The main aspects considered at this stage from an E&S perspective are summarized below. However, each project will have its own assessment completed during project preparation and implementation.

The following table provides a summary of the E&S categories and the World Bank Group (WBG) policies that apply to each category. Projects that are part of the Facility will be in Category A following the GCF classification and may be classified as high or substantial risk as per the World Bank E&S classification.


| ESS1: Assessment and Management of E&S Risks and Impacts | The projects are expected to bring substantial direct environmental benefits by increasing the deployment of renewable energy. Transitioning to a higher percentage of renewable energy in the electricity mix has been shown to reduce greenhouse gas emissions, air pollution, and use of water resources. The projects are likely to result in significant employment generation, including construction jobs, followed by operation and maintenance (O&M) jobs. In addition, the projects will provide opportunities for women. There are substantial opportunities to promote female employment (direct and indirect) through training and incentives. |
| --- | --- |
| ESS2: Labor and Working Conditions | Project workers are expected to be mobilized at all the projects. The EE/Implementing agencies will have to comply with the E&S requirements as presented in Labor Management Procedures (LMP) to be prepared for each project. |
| ESS3: Resource Efficiency and Pollution Prevention and Management | The downstream risks and impacts related to potential water scarcity (also considering seasonal water availability and any cumulative effects by multiple demands), the release of pollutants, waste generation, the management of disposal materials and hazardous wastes, impacts on communities, and resource use |

1) Depending on the final design of the projects to be financed out of Component 3, the World Bank Group (WBG) will provide guidance on the financial activities under these projects, in accordance with the WBG's relevant policies and procedures.


---
**Page 68**
---

efficiency will be assessed for each project. However, most of these potential impacts are low to moderate in intensity/significance and are reversible and localized in nature, and therefore can be easily mitigated.

During the construction phase of the solar and wind parks, there may be some potential negative environmental and social impacts including: air quality deterioration due to dust and exhaust emissions during construction activities. These impacts require appropriate assessments and management measures to contain them:

- **ESS4:** Community Health and Safety
ES5S requirements will be taken into account in the implementation of project's activities. While the construction, operation and decommissioning of solar/wind power plants has risks accompanying both normal operations and potential accidents, there have to date not been any accidents of the severity that have occurred in other forms of energy production (e.g. in hydropower). There is a possibility of noise emissions from the running of wind turbines and solar panels, as well as visual and acoustic nuisance etc. These impacts require appropriate assessment and management measures to contain them.

- **ESS5:** Land Acquisition, Restrictions on Land Use and Involuntary Resettlement
Land needs for solar and wind parks are substantial. Any potential land acquisition or land use restriction will be assessed and managed in a manner consistent with ES5S requirements, in order to avoid, minimize, reduce/migrate and compensate at replacement cost land acquisition based on due diligence and plans prepared in accordance with ES5S.

- **ESS6:** Biodiversity Conservation and Sustainable Management of Living Natural Resources
The proposed solar/wind projects, if not properly designed and sited, may result in loss, degradation or fragmentation of natural habitats and other impacts on biodiversity and living natural resources. Care will be taken to ensure that the impacts of such projects on natural habitats, terrestrial and aquatic ecosystems and species are well assessed and managed.

- **ESS7:** Indigenous Peoples/Sub-Saharan African Historically Underserved Traditional Local Communities
The presence of indigenous people in the project areas or their collective attachment to such areas will be separately examined by the World Bank in consultation with the national authorities. Once it is confirmed, consistency with ES5S objectives and requirements will be integrated into the design of the project and E&S documents, including the development of an indigenous peoples' development plan or other types of plans developed at the country and project context, and Free, Prior and Informed Consent in the circumstances specified in ES5S.

- **ESS8:** Cultural Heritage
Each project will screen the project area and assess potential impacts on cultural heritage, both tangible and intangible. The site selection for each project will be mindful of cultural heritage and the application of ES5S will be further assessed under each project.

# G.2 Gender assessment and action plan (max. 500 words, approximately 1 page)

The Facility aims to enhance female economic participation in the energy sector and create income-generating opportunities for women in project countries. Proposed interventions under the Gender Action Plan are both aligned with the World Bank Group Gender Strategy (2016-2021) [ii] advocating equality between men and women, and with the REI strategy, which aims to address gender-related risks in climate change actions. Employment opportunities created by large-scale solar and wind infrastructure projects often benefit men more than women, resulting in a significant employment gap in the energy sector. The Gender Action Plan will focus on addressing this gap through targeted interventions.

The following issues are those that will be tackled in a sustainable manner, raising awareness, improving gender policies, building capacities and generating knowledge on the program's differentiated impact on men and women, and promoting gender equality in project countries under the Facility highlighted the following gender gaps and their potential mitigation actions:

a. **Female employment and skill gaps in the energy sector:** The major barriers to women’s participation in the energy sector include social norms that confine women to unpaid household and care work, as well as a lack of access to education, training, and skills development opportunities for work worldwide. Worldwide, the REI industry employs only about 32 percent of women. Therefore, without a proactive focus on relational reform to promote female employment gender policy, men may benefit disproportionately from new employment opportunities across the REI value chain.


---
**Page 70**
---

b. Female employment in the areas of implementation of RE projects: RE projects are frequently located in remote areas with good solar/wind resources and large plots of land, but when living standards are below the country average. Women, if employed, are more exposed to lower-paid, lower-quality employment in the informal sector. Under the SRII approach, the socio-economic needs of women should be considered as part of the project design. This could include measures that may include investments in education and skill-building, for instance in traditional home-based activities or in new energy-related activities. Special attention will be paid to the promotion of local women-run businesses along the project development cycle.

c. Gender-based violence: women routinely experience sexual harassment and intimate partner violence in target countries, and the risk of gender-based violence may increase during the realization of large-scale RE projects. This risk will be mitigated through implementing mitigation and response approaches that are consistent with the SRII approach and in accordance with international standards such as the World Bank Group GBV practices. These interventions are expected to efficiently complement actions taken by local women and men and aim to create a safe environment at the project sites and in communities.

d. Women access to services as energy consumers among project countries with lower electrification rates and under-developed grids, women suffer from time-poverty and increased health risks due to lack of electricity for cooking and lighting. This is particularly true for female-headed households (FHH). Limited access to electricity among poor households, including female-headed households (FHH) can also be explained through affordability constraints regarding electric connection or use of energy appliances. Access to electricity can help reduce the time spent on household chores and labor burden of women, generate health and education benefits, increase female labor participation and incomes. For these reasons, the EEs will be required to (i) pay attention to women and meet their needs in terms of access to energy services; (ii) ensure that women are involved in stakeholders’ consultations to ensure women’s voice is heard and included in decision-making processes at all levels; (iii) design and operate/running initiatives to increase women’s knowledge about new forms of energy, and; (iv) promote FHH in the design of actions to assist poor households with connection charges.

The Facility will follow a systematic approach and provide cross-support to projects interventions with a focus on data collection, policy engagement, project level action and capacity building activities. The SRII approach is an integral part of the Program. The Project will be required to give precedence to the SRMI facility, as part of its mandate to strengthen women’s role in the energy sector as consumers, employers and investors. The Facility will encourage the recruitment of a balanced female-to-male employment ratio within the PMU team.

Obtaining stakeholders buy-in will be key in ensuring that interventions to promote women's employment and enhance their livelihoods are effectively implemented with measurable outcomes. Countries adherence to international agreements and national normative frameworks on gender equality.

## G.3. Financial management and procurement (max 500 words, approximately 1 page)
Each EE of loan/grant financing under the SRII Facility will be required to conduct financial management and procurement according to the World Bank policies and procedures.


---
**Page 72**
---

# Procurement

Each project supported by the SRM Facility will follow the World Bank’s Procurement Regulations. World Bank procurement staff provide implementation support to the EEs throughout the procurement process. They work with governments to achieve the highest bidding and contract management standards to get the best development results. Each project is developed and supervised by a World Bank team, which includes an FM specialist who monitors the EE’s financial arrangements and capacity to use funds for their intended purposes.

# Financial Management

Each project (with the exception of the guarantees supported by the Facility) is required to maintain financial management arrangements (including planning and budgeting, accounting, internal controls, funds transfer, reporting, and auditing arrangements of the EE and other entities responsible for project implementation) acceptable to the World Bank. For each task, the project team includes an FM specialist who monitors the EE’s financial arrangements and capacity to use funds for their intended purposes.

The EE is required to prepare a Financial Management Plan (preparation, supervision and implementation) in accordance with the Bank's operational policies and procedures (to work with partner countries to design financial management and disbursement arrangements that are consistent with the Bank’s policies). The FM specialist closely monitors the performance of the EE’s FM arrangements and their continued capacity to provide reasonable assurance that financing proceeds are being used for the intended purposes.

Financial reporting will also be required for each project and (with the exception of the guarantees) is specified in each project's Disbursement and Financial Information Letter (DFL). Unless otherwise agreed by the Bank, the EE must submit audited annual financial statement six months after the close of the fiscal year. Financial statements are typically prepared periodically with the assistance out by auditors with independence and objectivity acceptable to the Bank, under terms of reference acceptable to the World Bank.

# Secretariat’s assessment of FP163

## Proposal name: Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative (SRMI) Facility

### Accredited entity: World Bank

### Country/ies: Benin, Central African Republic (CAR), Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), Kenya, Mali, Namibia, and Uzbekistan

### Project/programme size: Large

## I. Overall assessment of the Secretariat

1. The funding proposal is presented to the Board for consideration with the following remarks:

| Strengths | Points of caution |
| --- | --- |
| SRMI proposes a streamlined process with a one-stop shop approach from technical assistance through to critical public investment and the financial coverage needed to enable private investments at scale. This will make a significant difference, particularly when countries are prioritizing scarce resources available for an energy transition post-COVID-19 focusing on healthcare. | The SRMI Facility and the projects for investment are at pre-concept stage; proper assurances, including implementation of safeguards, need to be in place to ensure the Facility will deliver the impact as indicated in the proposal. It would be important that all parties strictly comply with the agreed set of parameters in the term sheet throughout the process. |

2. The Board may wish to consider approving this funding proposal with the terms and conditions listed in the term sheet and addendum XVII, titled “List of proposed conditions and recommendations,” respectively.

## II. Summary of the Secretariat’s assessment


---
**Page 91**
---

2.1 Project background

Achieving global goals of climate change mitigation and universal access to electricity will require a significant share of variable renewable energy (VRE) used in global energy generation. According to the Sustainable Development Scenario of the International Energy Agency (World Energy Outlook, 2018), capacity to generate 950 gigawatts (GW) using solar photovoltaic (PV) and wind power is required by 2040 for a low-carbon pathway in developing countries. This represents an investment of USD 150 billion capital expenditure needed annually over the next two decades, with USD 37 billion per year in operational costs. Additional finance will have to be unlocked to complement the limited public funding available.

The falling technology costs make investment in renewable energy (RE) commercially viable in many countries and regions that are not yet on track to meet their targets needed to meet the Paris Agreement objectives and its potential. A range of constraints can obstruct RE deployment, including but not limited to: (i) lack of enabling policies at the national environment; (ii) the limited generation and transmission capacity of utilities; (iii) the high costs associated with RE technologies; (iv) the low renewable generation capacity on an ad hoc and rarely optimum basis from a systems perspective; (v) lack of adequate financial resources to support RE deployment; (vi) weak institutional framework that can obstruct the development of IPP-owned generation; (vii) weak competitive procurement capacity to select the independent power producers that has often led to power purchase agreements with high tariffs; (viii) limited availability of long-term, low-interest and competitive bidding; (ix) limited financial viability of off-takers, which resulted in poor creditworthiness of RE projects; (x) lack of adequate risk mitigation instruments for private sector investment; (xi) challenges to support uptake of more RE because of grid technical limitations and lack of flexibility in the grid.

The proposed SMI aims to address these challenges by creating an enabling environment that is more conducive to private investment while maximizing socioeconomic benefits. The SMI will focus on seven countries with high levels of poverty, inequality, and fragility. The programme proposes various de-risking instruments for each of the identified constraints. These include: (a) technical assistance to improve RE policy frameworks; (b) critical public investments and the coverage needed for risk mitigation to enable private investment at scale; (c) SMI offers climate financing for three core aspects:

- Technical assistance to help countries develop a data-based VRE target, implement a sustainable RE programme, and maintain robust procurement processes with transparent bidding.
- Critical public investments to enable integration of VRE, finance solar/wind park infrastructure, and increase access to electricity; and
- Risk mitigation instruments to cover residual risks perceived by private investors.

The SMI will focus on seven start-up countries: Ethiopia, Kenya, Mozambique, Botswana, Côte d'Ivoire, DRC, Ivory Coast, Mali, Namibia, and Uzbekistan. The seven countries were selected based on the willingness and interest of governments to engage in a dialogue with the SMI Facility about the challenges they face in the implementation of their nationally determined contribution (NDC) and low-emission development pathways. They are representative of different types of markets/energy sectors, with varying levels of institutional capacity, policy frameworks, financial resources, and so on. The success and lessons learned from these seven countries will enable the SMI approach to be scaled up across countries and facilitate unlocking sustainable RE markets for private investments.

Table 1 provides details of the proposed financing plan for the SMI Facility. The plan is financed by a combination of grants from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) and loans from Reconstruction and Development/International Development Association (IRD/RIDA) in the form of concessional finance.


---
**Page 92**
---

# Estimated financing plan of Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative

## Sources (USD million)
- **GCF grant:** 25
    - Technical assistance and project management cost in seven countries
- **GCF grant (30 to CAB):** 18
    - Finance to public infrastructure
        * Common infrastructure for solar/wind parks: 40
        * Grid upgrade/relinquish: 130
        * Solar PV: 60
- **GCF concessional loan:** 175
- **GCF reimbursable grant:** 30
    - Providing risk mitigation revenue (reimbursable grant) for RE projects
- **GCF guarantee:** 28
    - Providing guarantee for renewable energy connected private projects as necessary in Botswana and Namibia
- **World Bank ESMAP grant:** 18
- **IDA grant:** 65
- **IBRD concessional loans:** 450
- **IBRD concessional loans (USD):** 750.3

## Total (USD): 280

### Abbreviations:
* CAB = Central African Region, ESMAP = Energy Sector Management Assistance Programme, IBRD = International Bank for Reconstruction and Development, IDA = International Development Association

### Notes:
1. USD 290 million of funding contribution of USD 290 million is co-financed with USD 128 million concessional finance from the accredited entity (AE). On top of AE co-financing, the AE further proposes to mobilize an estimated USD 3.3 billion of private capital to implement the RE projects downstream.
2. The majority of GCF concessional financing (USD 128 million out of 206 million) will be derived from the World Bank and IDA concessional loans. This is a critical step in RE project integration and unlocking private sector finance for the RE plants that could make commercial sense, but are not yet financially viable.
3. The AE proposes to provide USD 18 million of technical assistance grant to support the institutional capacity of countries for energy planning, put in place the legal framework for IPP procurement, and develop a national renewable energy plan. This will also include social risk mitigation instruments for the development of solar PV, solar PV with battery storage (SIP), and mini-grid wind parks in support of investments in fishing and livestock selection projects.

### Additional Notes:
In addition to the concessional financing, the AE proposes to put in place a technical assistance grant of USD 22.5 million out of which GCF has been requested to provide USD 20 million. This will be used for capacity building and institutional strengthening to support the institutional capacity of countries for energy planning, putting in place the legal framework for IPP procurement, developing a national renewable energy plan, and social risk mitigation instruments for the development of solar PV, solar PV with battery storage (SIP), and mini-grid wind parks in support of investments in fishing and livestock selection projects.


---
**Page 93**
---

1. The programme is expected to contribute to the shift to low emission sustainable development pathways as summarized by the World Bank and proposed as follows:
   (a) Directly support the development of an estimated 2.4 GW of RE, (b) 1.7 GW of solar PV; (c)0.8 GW of concentrated solar plants (CSP); and (d) 0.3 GW of onshore wind. Of this, 2 GW will be built under the projects in the seven countries and the remaining 0.4 GW are expected to be built after the end of the projects. It will also enable the integration of another estimated 1.5 GW of RE, including 0.8 GW of solar PV, through the GEF-7 VRE integration analysis and grid investments.
   (b) Directly reduce greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions by an estimated 97 million tonnes of carbon dioxide equivalent (CO2e), made up of a reduction of 81 MtCO2e for the 2 GW RE built under the projects over the 25 years of their operation, and 16 MtCO2e for 0.4 GW RE built after the end of the projects. The remaining 37 MtCO2e is expected to be prepared under the SMI Facility projects but not built during their lifetime.
   (c) Unlock an estimated USD 3.3 billion of private investment, including USD 8 billion for LDCs, through the purchase of the projects and USD 0.4 billion for the 0.4 GW built after the end of the projects;
   (d) Provide access to clean low-cost renewable electricity to an estimated 4.2 million people through the projects.
   (e) Strengthen institutional and regulatory systems for planning and development; and
   (f) Reduce locking-in long-lived climate-vulnerable infrastructure by increasing resilience of grid and RE resources.

2.3.1 Implementation arrangements

The Facility will be managed by the World Bank. In respect of each project in the seven countries, the World Bank will carry out the diligence to ensure that the individual projects meet the agreed eligibility criteria (section B.3 of the funding proposal), which are fully compliant with GEF-7 and World Bank policies and procedures. The World Bank will provide support to the projects, including necessary governance, oversight and quality assurance in accordance with its policies.

The Facility will be category A/1 following the GCF classification, and the relevant environmental and social safeguards (ESS) disclosure at the facility level has been completed in accordance with GEF-7 and World Bank policies. The World Bank will provide a joint statement between GCF and the World Bank on the disclosure and communication process for approval of each individual project.

### 2.2 Component-by-component analysis

Component 1: Technical assistance (total cost: USD 22.5 million; GCF cost: USD 21.5 million)

Component 1 (technical assistance) focuses on the country/macro-level risks and aims at supporting the seven countries in developing a national RE strategy, including mainstreaming them to harness RE resources at scale.

The objective of this component is to set a sustainable long-term target for renewables based on concrete analysis for demand, available resources and grid flexibility and stability. These targets will be the basis for a realistic but also ambitious plan to deploy RE capacity in each country. The technical assistance will also support the seven countries to revise their generation plans and to identify the critical investment needs for

## Large-scale VRE Integration while Maintaining Grid Reliability and Resilience

The targets will then be integrated into revisions of the NDC to ensure that they are implementable.

### Component 2: Public Investments (total cost: USD 1,466 million; GCF cost: USD 296 million)

This public investment component focuses on the grid- and infrastructure-level risk. It has two subcomponents:

#### Subcomponent 1.2: Solar and/or wind park commons/Aligned infrastructure

The solar and/or wind park commons/aligned infrastructure, ranging from the evacuation line to basic elements such as fence, roads and street lighting. The facility will use the solar/wind park commons approach to reduce the cost of preparing feasibility studies and environmental and social risk mitigation instruments in support of project development. The alignment with existing transmission lines and land availability studies may include geophysical analysis of the land around the substation, topography and geomorphology, soil characteristics, vegetation cover, water bodies, solar radiation/wind measurement analyzers, all of which will be made available for bidders.

#### Subcomponent 1.3: YRE integration grid upgrades (including publicly owned transmission and distribution systems)

The YRE integration grid upgrades represent significant expansion and modernization of electricity grids. The programme will support grid reinforcements through the following four components: (i) reinforcement of existing transmission lines and capacity expansions; (ii) provision of equipment for smoothing the voltage plan, such as capacitors, reactors, and other devices to improve power quality in the alternating current transmission systems; and (iii) equipment for faster and more efficient switching between different grid configurations. The programme will also include modernizing existing generating stations, including updating their operating systems, and automatic generation control systems for generation units and grid operations. Much attention will also be paid to increasing the grid's resilience to the impacts of extreme weather conditions, putting the structural integrity of aging power infrastructure at risk.

Infrastructure investments in electricity grids are often not directly linked to this activity because upfront investments are usually borne by public parties and constitute an important part of the national energy sector development. This subcomponent also faces challenges for countries.

#### Subcomponent 1.4: Electrification to increase grid- and grid connection

This subcomponent aims to invest in solar/wind park commons/aligned infrastructure, providing access to 800,000 households across different countries. This subcomponent is critical to enable public


---
**Page 95**

Incentives to clean energy mini grids and connection to the grid, which will be enhanced by the other interventions under the programme.
