### 2.3.1 Reimbursable grant (Democratic Republic of Congo)

D. The DRC sector is characterised by its weak governance and regulatory framework, which has a negative impact on the investment process, which affects its ability to crowd in private sector finance. The lack of a proven track record with a successful revenue model is also an issue. However, the fact that there are no mini-grids in operation despite the existence of latent demand for electricity in underserved communities is well known, the expected return on investment is high and the cost of capital is low. Therefore, there has been a significant level of uncertainty. Market analysis shows that the first years of activity are critical because the behaviour of productive users is still difficult to anticipate, despite a good preparatory phase. The DRC sector will therefore benefit from an initial period of risk mitigation measures. Therefore, large mini-grids are commonly perceived as an investment risk, given the slow return on investment.

21. The USD 30 million of GCF reimbursable grant aims to mitigate this demand risk during the initial years of operation. It is expected that this risk mitigation instrument will be made available in the form of a loan or equity facility for the benefit of selected project developers. Bidders will be requested to submit a business plan covering estimated demand as well as ability to repay, and a financial model showing how the grant will be repaid. The reimbursable clients will allow the special purpose vehicle to reimburse the lenders and equity providers. The business plan should also include an indicative revenue forecast for the first 5 years of operation in order to ensure it is robust. If the verified level of revenue from customers is below the pre-agreed minimum, the grant will be repaid by the project developer. Otherwise, the grant will be triggered. The use of a reimbursable grant will not only crowd in private sector finance but will also reduce the risk premium, ultimately ensuring a more affordable tariff in DRC.

### 2.3.2 Guarantee instrument (Namibia and Botswana)

In the context of Botswana and Namibia, limited financial viability of the utilities (project developers) is an issue. The first reason for this is the high cost of electricity in higher PPA prices. GCF guarantees will be used to strengthen the PPPs for project developers to improve their ability to attract private sector finance. There are two types of guarantee: the first relating to regular payments under the RE PPA contract, and the second relating to long-term recovery of capital from the government. The first type of guarantee is a payment guarantee where revenue receives the regular payments owed by the public entity or revenue under the RE PPA contract on a timely basis, whereas the latter will cover a one-off payment by the government following the end of the project period. The amount of the guarantee will be determined at the time of approval of subprojects and will be based on the AEC’s diligence and further market studies.

26. This component provides fit-for-purpose risk mitigation instruments based on needs in the individual countries and is expected to unlock the huge potential of the private sector in the targeted sectors.

### Assessment of performance against investment criteria

#### 3.1 Impact potential Scale: High
As a mitigation project, the proposed reports reduction of GHG emissions in the seven countries will be based on the emission of the 2.0 GW RE built during the project implementation and an additional 0.9 MCO2eq resulting from the 0.4 GW RE investment that will be prepared but not built during the project implementation. These impact figures exclude the indirect GHG emissions associated with the construction, operation and decommissioning of the grid infrastructure and the VRE integration analysis funded by the GCF investment. The AE will use the theoretical methodology for calculating the avoided emissions from the connection of projects to the grid-connected RE projects. The AE agreed to provide the methodology for off-grid sub-projects that are not connected to the grid.

Adaptation co-benefits resulting from the Facility include access to clean electricity provided to an additional 4.2 million people, including in areas where the population is under severe threat of flooding.

The programme's impact potential in the long term is notable. Impacts resulting from direct and indirect investments under the Facility are further broken down in the proposal; therefore, it is not possible at this stage to provide a quantification of the GHG emission reduction at this moment if any of the GHG emission reductions achieved by the programme will be converted into carbon credits. The AE agreed with the GCF that in the absence of universally acceptable methodology on attribution, it was agreed with the AE that in case that the Facility is able to achieve the 10% carbon offset emissions reduction achieved by the Facility has to seek consent of GCF, which the secretariat will continue to explore plausible ways to deal with this matter for future transactions.

#### 3.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High
The key pillar of the programme's paradigm shift potential lies in the comprehensive approach taken to mitigate the risks identified in the renewable development sector encountered by countries in their efforts to scale up RE investments and to unlock the maximum private financing for a capital expenditure-intensive sector.

At the strategic level, the SBM aims at supporting countries to transform their strategy to leverage the full potential of RE. The SBM will support countries in scaling up resources on scale. The SBM will assist countries to set long-term RE targets, based on concrete analysis of demand and feed-in tariffs. These targets are expected to be incorporated into the NDC revisions to guide countries' priority in subsequent phases.

In terms of the grid- and infrastructure-level RE, SBM, through its public investment component, will support countries in developing a robust transmission system which contributes to achieving a fully cost-effective electricity tariff and can effectively mitigate the risk of congestion. The SBM will also support countries in preparing their grid expansion and modernization based on countries' least-cost transmission plans to support the integration

It will increase the energy security of the countries, diversifying their energy mix and improving their energy access levels. At the community level, it is expected that the SMIH will benefit mine grids and household services it is expected to bring in considerable environmental and health benefits, including reduced air pollution.

Sites with good solar/wind resources and large-scale development potential are expected to generate significant employment at a rate above the national average. The tailor-made development programmes offered by the Facility will ensure that local communities benefit from the creation of jobs and development of skills, as well as increased resilience and enhanced livelihoods.

# Needs of the recipient

Although the seven countries have different economic development levels, they are commonly facing budget constraints on their budgets due to the COVID-19 pandemic and are likely to opt for a debt recovery strategy that would promote fossil fuel generation unless there is a strong policy commitment to support renewable energy deployment. The GCF aims at harnessing renewables through the best use of limited public finance. Private investments are a means to deploy renewables, which are generally more capital intense. To ensure that the trade-off between private and public investment is transparent and fair, the SMI framework (which may be more expensive) is fair for the SMI. SMI aims at bringing down the cost of green projects by providing a comprehensive and integrated risk mitigation framework that is able to leverage climate finance.

### Assessment of consistency with GCF safeguards and policies

#### Environmental and social safeguards

The SGRF Facility aims to support countries in developing sustainable solar and wind programs that will help to promote energy access and reduce reliance on public finance, while mainstreaming the socio-economic benefits triggered by the projects deployed. It will finance several project categories: (i) [private sector] investments in renewable energy infrastructure; (ii) technical assistance grants to provide comprehensive and planning and advisory support; (iii) public investments in VRE infrastructures, in the form of solar and wind parks, wind and solar power plants, and other related projects; (iv) risk mitigation instruments for private sector renewable energy projects; and (v) risk mitigation instruments, which would provide risk mitigation instruments to enable private sector investment in renewable energy infrastructure projects. Each project will individually go through the World Bank project cycle process, including adequate environmental and social approval of a project proposal, as well as the criteria established in each country.

#### Environmental and Social Safeguards (ESS) Risk category

The Facility is proposed as Category A. The Sectoral Environment category applies, and it is within the AE’s assessment level. The investment programs expected to be funded under the programme include large solar PV and CSP plants/arrays, wind farms, and other renewable energy infrastructure projects. These are also expected to support solar home systems, mini-grids and grid connection. The larger solar and wind projects will have significant land use impacts, including the acquisition of land for the project sites and having involuntary resettlement issues involving formal and informal land occupants/settlers. Wind turbines may also have visual impacts on their surroundings, as well as potential impacts in their vicinity, negatively impacting birds; and, if built near the shore/shelf/adjacent to areas affecting the marine ecosystem, economic activities and livelihoods, as well as cause coastal erosion due to alterations of sediment transport patterns. The construction of solar plants may also involve the handling of hazardous waste materials. Associated facilities and auxiliary infrastructure will have their own environmental and social impacts.

#### Safeguards instrument and disclosures

The Facility will cover seven countries, which would have different levels of implementation capacity. A specific scope of the country projects has been provided in the funding proposal, but the specific projects to be funded would have to be identified through a project identification process. As part of this process, a document titled Environmental and Social Sustainability Framework (ESSF). This document describes how the SGRF Facility will address environmental and social issues associated with each country project under its Environmental and Social Framework (ESF). It includes indicative outcomes for each dimension of the environmental and social impact assessment, Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework, Gender Specific Policy Framework and various other instruments for individual projects.

The World Bank ESF requires that a document titled Environmental and Social Standards (ESS) (ESS1-ESS8 and ESS10), which fully covers the GCF environmental and social safeguards standards as well as the stakeholder engagement and grievance redress mechanism


---
**Page 100**
---

requirements. The following describes how the programme would comply with these standards and requirements at the project level.

### ESS 1: Assessment and management of environmental and social risks and impacts

The All has highlighted that the projects under the programme are expected to bring substantial benefits in terms of energy security, reduction of greenhouse gas emissions, air and water pollution, and use of water resources, employment and economic development. However, these projects may also have adverse effects on the environment such as noise from power plants, parks, as well as the construction and operation of the RE power plants, may result in adverse environmental impacts including but not limited to land degradation, soil erosion, habitat risk categorization, ESS assessment and mitigation planning commensurate to its size and complexity, in accordance with the relevant national legislation, policies, guidelines, and standards. Environmental and social risks and impacts relating to ESS1 and ES510. The assessments and management of ESS risks and impacts for each project will have to take into consideration the country context, including but not limited to the environmental and social conditions in the country and local environmental and social conditions. The exact roles and responsibilities will be defined in the environmental and social commitment plan developed by the borrower/EE. This plan will also include a description of how the ESS risks and impacts will be managed as well as any necessary capacity-building activities on ESS aspects.

### ESS 2: Labour and working conditions

The projects to be funded by the Facility are expected to contribute positively to the objectives and requirements of ESS1 in terms of labour relations, discrimination and other unfair labour practices, occupational health and safety, and worker participation. The borrower/EE will prepare an employment and social management plan (ESMP), each project that would involve the hiring of workers will be required to prepare a separate occupational health and safety plan as per the World Bank Group’s Occupational Health and Safety Policy. The ESMP will also include a grievance mechanism, which will also provide for the establishment of a dedicated grievance redress mechanism for project workers. The borrower/EE will ensure that the employment and social management procedure will be reflected in the bidding and contractual documents of project components.

### ESS 3: Resource efficiency and pollution prevention and management

The proposed Facility will contribute positively to the objectives and requirements of ESS1 in terms of resource efficiency, energy conservation investments, best practices of BE, and GHG reduction benefits with focus on waste minimization, water conservation, and recycling. The environmental and mitigation plans will cover the downstream risks and impacts related to resource use and pollution prevention and management (e.g., air emissions (especially seasonal effects of multiple demand); noise; and air emissions; soil waste generation; management of disposal of waste materials and hazardous waste). These risks are expected to be mitigated through the design, construction, and operations of the infrastructure facility. However, most of these potential impacts are expected to be avoided by the borrower/EE in its project components.

### ESS 4: Community health, safety and security

ESS4 requirements will be taken into account in the design and implementation of individual projects. The ESS assessments and mitigation/management plans will consider the effects on community health, safety and security, which may include: potential spread of infectious diseases; increase in criminality, alcoholism and DWI; increased risk of accidents and injuries to workers and other members of the workforce, including migrant workers, and other security-related issues. Moreover, the ESS assessments and mitigation/management planning will also consider the effects of nuisance and pollution from


---

constructs activities on the residents' health and welfare. All measures under the responsibility of the contractors, such as management of construction traffic, ensuring safety of residents at construction sites, control of noise and dust, generation of waste, water screening of earthen materials, and good relations with communities will be reflected in the bidding and contract documents.

### ESS 5: Land acquisition, restrictions on land use and involuntary resettlement.
Land requirements for project activities will be identified. Environmental and social impact assessments (ESIA) and land-use restrictions will be assessed and managed in a manner consistent with ESIS requirements. The assessment of land requirements will include the identification of land acquisition based on the diligence and plans prepared in accordance with ESS5. An indicative note of the amount of land required for project activities is included in the programme's ESF. The EIA in the borrower country will be responsible for preparing a report that is deemed satisfactory to the World Bank, the project-specific resettlement policy frameworks and/or resettlement action plans (RAPs) will be prepared.

### ESS 6: Biodiversity conservation and sustainable management of living natural resources.
Solar and wind projects. If not properly designed and sited, may result in loss, degradation or fragmentation of biodiversity. The borrower country will ensure that the project area is free from any existing protected areas and that no new protected areas are created within the project area. Care will be taken to ensure that the impacts of such projects on natural biodiversity are minimized through appropriate mitigation measures and managed. All projects will include measures to ensure full compliance with ES6.

### GCF indigenous peoples policy and ESS 7: Indigenous peoples' sub-Saharan Africa.
African countries will provide a list of indigenous peoples in their country, including those in the project areas or their collective attachment to such areas will be screened and confirmed. The borrower country will confirm that the project area is free from any existing protected areas and that no new protected areas are created within the project area. If the project area is confirmed, consistency with ESST objectives and requirements will be integrated into the design of the project. A detailed indigenous peoples' plan or other types of plans depending on the country and project context, and free, prior, and informed consent (FPIC) will be required to be included in ESS7. An Indigenous Peoples Planning Framework is provided as part of the ESF.

### ESS 8: Cultural heritage.
The EF of the borrower country will serve the project area and assess cultural heritage impacts. The assessment will include a review of existing laws, selection for each project will be mindful of cultural heritages, and the application of ES8 will be further guided by the World Bank's policy on cultural heritage. The implementation plan based on existing country laws and regulations, and on international good practice, and will be included as part of the ESS.

### Gender policy

The AE has provided a gender assessment and gender action plan, and therefore complies with the requirements of the GCF Gender Policy.

The AE has provided information on the levels of enabling environment that exist in all seven countries covered by the project, as well as their economic and social development. An overview of each country's economic and social development reveals the different stages each of these countries are at but also demonstrates, regardless of the differences, that all share face gaps in addressing gender equality and women's empowerment.

Some countries are more unequal than others and provide limited socioeconomic opportunities for women compared to men.

At the national level, most of the countries are signatories of, for example, the Convention on the Elimination of All Forms of Discrimination Against Women, and the Beijing Declaration and Platform for Action. At least, most have now adopted national strategies and mechanisms to promote gender equality. While national-level commitments exist in many of the countries covered by this project, there is a need to ensure that all available resources and girls can make in the social, economic and political spheres.

In the energy sector, the assessment indicates that women suffer most from insufficient access to electricity and clean cooking fuels and technologies, which are mostly responsible for household chores. Moreover, women are found to have limited employment opportunities in the formal sector, including in the renewable energy sector. The assessment also indicates that women's Limited political participation and representation results in lower levels of female decision-making power at all levels of government. In addition, women's access to education is low, with the electrification rate stands at less than 48 percent, leaving nearly 600 million people without access to electricity. This has a direct impact on women's health, nutrition, and their decision-making, education and health outcomes. The assessment indicates that, every year, 4.3 million people—mostly women and children—as a result of indoor air pollution. Overall, the level of access to electricity is low in all countries covered by this project. In fact, it ranks among the lowest countries in terms of overall access to electricity. For example, Kenya is the only country that has achieved universal access to electricity (98 percent), while 10 other countries have access to less than 50 percent of their population. The Democratic Republic of Congo and especially women relying on fuelwood and charcoal for energy. Worldwide, only about 32 percent of people in rural areas have access to modern fuels and technologies for cooking (UNDP, 2019). On the other hand, access to electricity stands at around it percent in the Central African Republic, Chad, and Niger. The Democratic Republic of Congo has a very low electrification rate, with only about 35 percent of people having access to modern fuels and technologies for cooking (UNDP, 2019).

### 4.3 Risks

#### 4.3.1 Overall programme assessment (medium-high risk)

The project aims at tackling the lack of sustainable and bankable pipeline for RE projects in developing countries to achieve low-emission development pathways. The project consists of three main components: (i) technical assistance, including training on energy efficiency, renewable energy technologies, and solar and wind park preparatory studies; (ii) loans and grants for public investments in solar and wind power generation capacity; and (iii) reimbursable grants and guarantees for risk mitigation instruments for mini grids and grid-connected projects.

The total project cost is USD 1.56 billion, of which USD 280 million is GCF finance. GCF financing consists of USD 84 million in grants (of which USD 30 million is reimbursable), USD


---
**Page 104**
---

174 million loans and USD 20 million in guarantees. Co-financing is provided by several World Bank entities. IBRD will provide USD 466 million on senior loans; IDA will provide USD 750 million loan and USD 60 million grant. To achieve the impact as presented in the funding proposal, additional financing beyond what has been committed or can be sourced from other sources is required. This additional financing is beyond the scope of the project and not arranged by the AE. This leads to increased uncertainty about the impact materializing as envisaged.

### 4.3.2 Accredited entity/executing entity capability to execute the current programme (medium risk)

The World Bank is the AE for the proposed programme. Given the structure of the programme, the broad eligibility criteria, and limited visibility at this stage regarding the end projects, it may be difficult to determine whether the AE has sufficient capacity to deliver all AE functions.

For the purposes of the loans and grants, the respective countries represented through Ministry of Energy (MoE) are considered as executing entities. For the purposes of the guarantees, the private sector RE project-companies will be the EEs. Thus, some of the potential risks associated with the programme may not be fully mitigated by the AE during the appraisal stage of each project. In assessing the capacities of the RE EE AE will apply a risk-based approach to determine whether there are any technical, financial management, procurement and ESS capacities for implementation.

In addition to the EEs, the project is dependent on private sector entities for developing the entire portfolio of projects. The AE will need to monitor the progress of project grant strengthening activities. The project's impact is contingent on the execution and operational capacity of the private sector entities.


### 4.3.3 Programme-specific risks (high risk)

72 Impact risk: direct impact as presented in the funding proposal (A5). Expected mitigation measures are that the projects will have one RE capacity of 2 GW operating over 25 years. The impact quantum will be reduced if the construction of RE projects or the operation of RE projects is delayed.

### Carbon credits: while the FP estimates an impact of 89 million tCO2eqT, the impact is exposed to high risk of dilution. The term sheet proposed by AE provides that if the EEs seek to convert carbon credits into RE certificates, they will be required to pay a premium to the Fund in exchange with GCF on volume of impact directly attributable to GCF which may not be converted into other credits. In addition, the AE has clarified that the Fund will not put any restriction on offsetting impact not attributable to GCF, and which would be included in the 89 million tCO2eqT. The term sheet proposed by AE provides that the carbon credits impact will be generated at the private sector RE generating projects level and not at EE level; the term sheet does not impose any restriction on the private sector RE generating projects to generate offsetting impact. Also, the clarification provided by AE in the funding proposal impact report to the Fund is that the AE clarifies in its funding proposal impact report that the carbon credits will be deducted from the impact reported to the Fund it is advised that the AE clearly provides in the funding proposal impact report that the carbon credits will be deducted from the impact reported to the Fund.

#### Project viability and concessionality

The AE proposes providing a high concessionary loan with equal terms for all seven countries, which are at different levels of RE development. There are also meaningful differences in terms of credit ratings between the countries, for example, India has an investment grade (Moody’s) while China has had an investment grade rating A2 by Moody’s. Given the current limitations of the financial aid and the significant differences between the seven countries, it is advised that the AE considers differentiation in financing terms across countries and apply these terms in line with the minimum concessionality principle.

#### Compliance risk (high risk)

When asked whether it had conducted any assessment of risks to the project, the AE (World Bank) stated that it had not done so. The AE has acknowledged that it has not conducted any risk assessment specific to this proposed action. Whether or not the AE has conducted a risk assessment is of no relevance and provides an answer which is informed Compliance Risk rating for this project. In light of specific information directed toward this project, the Compliance Risk rating is rated as “high.”

#### GCF portfolio concentration risk (low risk)

In case of approval, the impact of this proposal on the GCF portfolio concentration in terms of result area and single proposal is not material.

### 4.4 Fiduciary

- As the AE, the World Bank will be responsible for the management and administration of GCF resources and will carry out such in accordance with its policies, procedures and practices as set forth in the agreement between the AE and the World Bank (the accreditation master agreement (AMA)). The World Bank will apply its own fiduciary principles and standards relating to any integrity checks, anti-corruption, countering the financing of terrorism, fraud, financial management and procurement.
- During implementation of the Facility, the World Bank will be responsible for providing the necessary support to the AE in accordance with its policies, procedures and any specific requirements in the AMA and FAA.
- The World Bank team will manage and be responsible for the Facility's overall objective achievement and the project results. The World Bank will monitor progress. The World Bank will inform the GCF Secretariat about projects selected in accordance with the eligibility criteria as part of the Due Diligence process.
- In addition to the Facility-supported projects (project implementation through completion and evaluation) follows the World Bank's applicable policies and procedures. For each project, the AE must appoint a team lead, technical expert, procurement specialist, financial management specialist, and external auditor. The AE will be responsible for its own monitoring and evaluating and reporting process, and the EE will be accountable for its implementation.
- Each EE of disbursement funding under the SBMI Facility will be required to conduct financial management and procurement according to the World Bank policies and procedures. Financial statements (including a statement on the status of guarantees) must be submitted as specified in each project's Disbursement and Financial Information Letter. Unless otherwise agreed by the Bank, the EE must submit annual audited project financial statements at least once every three years. The AE will also be responsible for submitting its own periodic audits carried out by auditors with independence and capacity acceptable to the Bank, under terms of reference acceptable to the World Bank.

### Legal assessment

The accreditation master agreement was signed with the AE on 13 November 2017 (the "AMAG"), and entered into force on 1 February 2019.

The AE has provided a legal opinion/certificate confirming that it has obtained all internal approvals and that it has the capacity and authority to implement the programme. Proposals for implementation will be implemented in Botswana, Namibia, the Central African Republic, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Kenya, Mali, Namibia and Uzbekistan.

GCF has signed a bilateral agreement on privileges and immunities with Namibia.

GCF is not provided with privileges and immunities in the six other countries. This means that, among other things, GCF is not protected against litigation or expropriation in such countries.

With respect to Botswana, the discussion on the privileges and immunities agreement with the Government of Botswana has not yet started.

With respect to the Central African Republic, the discussion on the privileges and immunities agreement with the Government of the Central African Republic has not yet started.

With respect to the Democratic Republic of the Congo, the Secretariat submitted a draft privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of the Democratic Republic of the Congo on 10 October 2018 and a bilingual version on 1 March 2019. The agreement is currently under review by the Government.

With respect to Kenya, the Secretariat submitted a first draft privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of Kenya in July 2017. Following several rounds of negotiations, the Government responded that the revised draft privileges and immunities agreement required further review. The agreement is currently under review by the Government.

With respect to Mali, the Secretariat submitted a first draft privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of Mali on 3 October 2018. A bilingual version was sent to the Government on 21 December 2017. A meeting was held at the GCF Global NDA Conference on 9 October 2018. The agreement is currently under review by the Government.

With respect to Namibia, the Secretariat submitted a first draft privileges and immunities agreement to the Government of Uzbekistan on 10 December 2018. The agreement is currently under review by the Government.

Independent Technical Advisory Panel's assessment of FP163

Proposal name: Sustainable Renewables Risk Mitigation Initiative (SRRM) Facility

Accredited entity: World Bank

Country/ies: Botswana, Central African Republic (CAR), Democratic Republic of the Congo (DRC), Kenya, Mali, Namibia, and Uzbekistan

Project/programme size: Large


I. Assessment of the independent Technical Advisory Panel

1.1 Impact potential Scale: High

1. In 2015, Parties to the United Nations Framework Convention for Climate Change (UNFCCC) came together under the Paris Agreement to transform their development trajectories so that the world can proceed on a path to sustainable development, which aims at limiting global warming to well below 2°C and pursuing efforts to limit it even further. The strategies of each country are designed to achieve this goal in reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions. The Agreement requires countries to submit Nationally Determined Contributions (NDCs), which present the strategies to achieve this goal in reducing greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions, and climate-resilient development. Nationally determined contributions (NDCs) are prepared and submitted to the UNFCCC by all the signatory countries. The strategies that each country will implement to meet its stated mitigation objective will be implemented only if unconditional interventions are stipulated in their NDC. The developing countries included in this proposed World Bank (WB) intervention are expected to clearly spell out domestic measures that they will take to achieve the objective of the proposed contributions.

2. The NDC of each country is expected to clearly spell out domestic measures that countries will take to achieve the objective of the proposed contributions. The contributions of the 195 Parties to the Paris Agreement are not unconditional, and it remains for the parties to determine whether the long-term goals of the Paris Agreement are achieved and greenhouse gas (GHG) emissions are reduced in line with the objectives set out by the agreement. It is also expected that there will be a balance between anthropogenic emissions by sources and removals by sinks of GHGs in the second half of this century, so as to achieve the long-term temperature goal of the Paris Agreement.

3. The seven countries included in this proposed World Bank (WB) intervention are all conditional to having international financial support; and those that can implement themselves (not conditional to having unconditional funding). All seven countries that are covered by this proposal have submitted their NDCs to the UNFCCC. The proposed countries include: Botswana, Central African Republic (CAR), Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Kenya, Mali, Namibia, and Uzbekistan. The SRRM Facility aims to be scaled up by adding countries, namely Indonesia and Vietnam. These countries will be added if they meet the same eligibility criteria as per the term sheet and the present funding proposal.

4. The seven countries submitted an NDC in the UNFCCC that unconditional interventions are required for them to achieve their stated mitigation objective. While unconditional interventions contained in a country's NDC will be affected by the availability of public and private


---
**Page 111**
---

investments can also be argued that conditional measures, which will heavily depend on the availability of international financial support, may also require some level of public funding that will need as counterpart funding. According to implicit information derived from the funding gap analysis in the NDCs, it is estimated that around 10% of the total budget for the NDC projects through allocation of public funding prior to the shutdown of economies caused by COVID-19 pandemic. This implies that the countries with limited domestic resources may have to limit the availability of funds for NDC intervention thus making it almost impossible to achieve their climate goals in a timely manner.

Renewable energy systems (RES) include: solar and wind— together referred to as variable renewable energy (VRE); biomass and waste-to-energy; geothermal; marine; and small hydro, many of which are considered low carbon. The share of RES in the total electricity mix is suitable to deliver a low carbon future and can enable the project countries to contribute their quota towards achieving their climate goals under the Paris Agreement. Prior to the COVID-19 pandemic, many countries, including the developing countries, were on track to meet their commitments under the Paris Agreement for mitigation. These NDC documents clearly stipulated actions that will be implemented by the countries with funding support from international financial institutions and other donors. The impact of the country level will be conditional on having, international support in place to fund low carbon projects.

Another pertinent observation made in the funding proposal is that a significant proportion of the energy sectors of these seven countries is currently supplied by coal, with plans for capacity expansion. According to the International Energy Agency (IEA), the proposed installed coal capacity in the seven countries, is expected to increase from 0.5 GW to 7 GW between now and 2030 as per their current plans; by 2018, 8.5 GW of solar and wind generation was already in place in these countries. The IEA projects that this will be a 90 per cent of total mix. When this share is estimated using GW energy production, the solar and wind share would be even higher. According to the International Energy Agency and estimates by the International Energy Agency and estimations by the World Bank, around 20 GW and 45 GW of renewable energy capacity will need to be installed in these seven countries and other developing countries by 2025 and 2030 respectively, as part of their electricity mix according to their NDCs and IEA. This would require an investment of around USD 15 billion for new solar and wind generation alone by 2025.

While the implementation of RES has been recognised as an important strategy for achieving climate goals, it is not without challenges. The main challenge in implementing implementation of low carbon systems. The limited installed capacity of cost-competitive solar and wind energy technologies in these countries is a result of financial, regulatory, structural and technical constraints. The financial constraints include the scarcity of public funds to finance continuous debt-financing of power transmission infrastructure and grid expansion, which are critical for RES integration. For example, the existing infrastructure usually requires continuous maintenance and technical strengthening to ensure its reliability and efficiency in order to support the power grids. Without such interventions it is difficult to develop grid connected VRE systems. The above constraints are likely to be exacerbated by the economic impact of COVID-19 on the development of large RES systems in the project countries and may be compounded by the COVID-19 pandemic.

Currently these countries are prioritising the immediate emergency response to manage the current COVID-19 crisis and public investments are being channelled to address the impacts of the crisis. The economic impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on the seven countries is likely to be crucial in shaping the long-term pathways for emissions and determining whether they will be able to meet their NDC targets. This will also have implications for development strategies and policies in response to the coming economic crisis, emissions could rebound and even overshoot the previously projected levels by 2030, despite lower economic


---
**Page 112**
---

# Growth in the same period. Stated in another way, although the seven countries have ambitious GHG emission reduction objectives as part of their NDCs, their current energy policies, the planned deployment of coal and the COVID-19 crisis may inhibit these NDC achievements.

## To tackle exacerbation by the pandemics of existing risks, the World Bank (the accredited entity/AE) has proposed to use funds from the SBI trust fund to support a Green Climate Fund project to support the initiative in Botswana, Central African Republic (CAR), Democratic Republic of Congo (DRC), Eswatini, Malawi, Mozambique and Zambia. The overall objective of this project programme is described above and the proposal was reviewed by the independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) of the funding proposal and its annexes. Following TAP's comments and related annexes, the project has been developed around four major components, summarized as follows:

### Component 1: Technical assistance

- The independent Technical Advisory Panel (TAP) review of the funding proposal and its annexes indicated that this component will focus on the following:
    - Capacity-building for the planning of renewable energy least-cost generation plans combined with VRE integration analysis and dispatch capacity-building for planning.
    - Capacity-building for the legal, financial and procurement aspects and transaction advisory support to implement competitive and transparent tendering of privately-financed solar projects.
    - Technical and environmental and social studies to prepare solar and wind parks for future tenders.

### Component 2: Public investments

- The World Bank will concentrate on public investment for:
    - Solar and/or wind park commons/shared infrastructure;
    - VRE integration grid upgrades (including publicly owned battery storage) and enhancing grid resilience to climate change; and
    - Electrification to enhance the resiliency of populations.

### Component 3: Risk mitigation instruments

- Activities at this component level will concentrate on providing risk mitigation instruments to private investors for grid-connected and off-grid renewable energy/storage projects, including solar and wind power generation. The World Bank will focus its efforts on the residual risks for private investors aiming to ensure sustainable leveraging of private investment in both on-grid and off-grid RE projects in the seven countries.

### Component 4: Project management

- This component will concentrate on the management of the overall coordination and implementation of the project, including compliance with the funding entities' (FEs) and programme implementing entities' (PIEs) contractual obligations included or referred to in the legal agreements entered into with the accredited entities (AEs).

The costs associated with the electricity generation projects delivered by this project as estimated and presented in the funding proposal and the annexes submitted for this review have been evaluated by the independent TAP as conservative estimates. These are summarized below:

- In addition to the 2.1 GW RE capacity, the SBMI will finance up to an additional 0.4 GW of RE capacity that is expected to be delivered directly by the project in the seven countries that will be covered by the SBMI. These are expected to be built during the lifetime of the SBMI program.
- It has also been estimated that the implementation of the proposal that the SBMI will result in the preparation of an implementation plan for 400 MW of VRE, which are expected to be delivered under the SBMI program.

The SBMI Facility will also enable the integration of another 1.9 GW of RE. The integration of other projects will be enabled through the VRE integration analysis that will be conducted as part of components 1.3 and component 2.2 under which core grid infrastructure will be financed.
- The SBMI Facility, through the projects described above, will unlock USD 3.3 billion of private sector investment in RE projects over a period of approximately five years with the lifetime of the projects and USD 300 million for the 0.4 GW that will be built after the end of the SBMI program.
- The SBMI Facility will also enable the integration of other projects through the activities of component 1.2 that will deliver transaction advisory and bankable project development services to governments in the seven countries, as well as SBMI support of technical assistance to governments under Component 1. In addition to this, the critical infrastructure under Component 2 and guarantees and risk mitigation instruments under Component 3 are expected to enable private sector investment in VRE in seven countries.
- The total emission reductions are based on the methodologies used in the calculation of Gg emission reductions expected to be delivered by these SBMI components. The methodological approach in all the calculations presented in the submission were judged as conservative and therefore, the results should not be taken as definitive estimates of Gg emission reductions. The total mitigation potential of the SBMI measured against the baseline has been reliably estimated to be about 97.5 million metric tCO2eq made up of:
- (0) 89.0 million metric tCO2eq reduction for the 2.1 GW RE that will be built under the SBMI.
- (0) 8.5 million metric tCO2eq for the 0.4 GW of RE projects that will be prepared under these projects but not built during their lifetime.

In addition to the emission reductions and other benefits above, the project will provide access to clean low-emission electricity to 4.2 million people through the electrification investments that will be engendered by the SBMI interventions.

# 1.2 Paradigm shift potential Scale: High

## 1.2.3 Comprehensiveness

In the absence of this SRMI intervention, the following baseline conditions will continue to hold in all the countries covered:

- The ability of countries to take electricity from VRE will be very constrained.
- The development of VRE systems will be very constrained.
- Most of the solar and wind power systems that many of the seven countries relied on for their energy needs are not yet operational.

An important outcome of the situation described in (a) above is that in many of the seven countries covered by the SRMI, especially those with baseline coal power plants and good access to VRE resources, the ability of countries to meet their long-term emissions reduction targets will be ramped up with strong negative consequences for the ability of countries to meet the target. In the absence of this SRMI intervention, the target goal of these countries to limit global warming to between 1.5°C and 2°C above pre-industrial levels will not be met.

In the absence of this SRMI intervention, there will be scarce and consequently investments in power capacity additions and the preparation of national grids for VRE will fall short.

The in-country capacity to carry out renewable power transactions, plan capacity expansion, regulate and create an enabling market environment for power systems' capacity expansion and the development of a robust grid infrastructure is also at risk. The COVID-19 pandemic has severely affected the world economy and exacerbated the situations of the seven project countries. The availability of public funding has been further constrained by the pandemic, which will make it more difficult for these countries in achieving their intended NDCs and making enhanced energy access a reality.

During the assessment the independent TAP discussed project risks with the SRMI. The AE explained that the SRMI intervention is expected to mitigate all identified SRMI interventions as summarized below:

The independent TAP noted that a number of international (bilateral and multilateral) agencies, including the World Bank, are already working in each of the seven nations on issues similar to the proposed SRMI intervention (strengthening of existing grid to take VRE capacity, development of new transmission lines, expansion of solar and wind projects, etc.). The AE pointed out that the risk of double-counting could be eliminated by ensuring that all parties involved are aware of each other's activities at an internal high-level platform – to discuss SRMI core principles and hence share each other’s vision and priorities. This will also ensure that any overlaps or gaps in funding and implementation synergies are enhanced. The AE also pointed out that the individual projects to be


---
**Page 116**
---

funded under the SMI programme will be selected according to GCF project metrics and criteria thereby ensuring that there is no duplication.

### A second type of risk raised during the independent TAP discussions with the AE is lack of the necessary public and private investment for VRE in each of these countries as a result of the high cost of capital. This is particularly true where existing power generation infrastructure as public financing is being diverted to meet the needs of critical health and sanitation services, which are often prioritized over energy access. The SMI aims to address payment issues from consumers, for example reduced demand leading to an increased risk premium on private investment in VRE. This will be achieved by reducing the premium on the cost of capital and increasing the power purchase agreement (PPA) price.

The World Bank has proposed a number of measures to support this objective:

- The SMI has been designed to support first the governments of the seven countries to leverage private investments in optimal conditions, while maximizing socioeconomic benefits. This is achieved by providing public financing for projects that are economically intensive - without impacting public finances.
- The main constraint with having private investment in VRE is the high cost of capital and the limited availability of long-term debt financing. However, to ensure that the trade-off between public financing (that is limited and costly but better) and private financing (that may be more expensive) is fair for the seven countries, the SMI will provide a range of financial instruments to support renewable energy generation through an integrated risk mitigation framework leveraging climate finance by reducing the cost of capital.
- The World Bank proposed to minimize public investment to those critical assets that will be publicly owned and operated. These include transmission lines, distribution networks as medium-term evidence-based plan and independent power producers (IPPs) are selected for private sector participation. The SMI will provide a range of financial intermediation instruments available to reduce further the residual risk (political, utility payments, etc.), and;
- The independent TAP identified a further residual risk in the possibility of not having an adequate number of IPPs to bring in private investment for VRE in some of the countries. To address this issue, the SMI will provide financial instruments that can be utilized to de-risk existing power grids so as to facilitate the uptake of renewable power investment from private investors; availability of guarantees as tools for risk mitigation to the private sector is a key element of the SMI. The SMI will also provide institutional support for public sector stakeholders in the power sector of each of the nine countries for planning and implementation of VRE projects. If the seven countries are not able to attract and finance capable IPPs in some of the seven countries, then the de-risking of existing power grids, which is a key element of the SMI to institutionalize grids, will not be realized. In response to the issue of IPP investments, the AE pointed out that the SMI will provide financial instruments for VRE projects.

In order to deliver comprehensive outcomes, competition among IPPs must be engineered. The SMI will call for competitive bidding, operating under a framework act low-cost capital and a transparent process for selecting IPPs. This could only happen if the IPP engagement process is open and competitive. The World Bank expects an open and competitive process for IPP selection to be already in place or to be established by the time the SMI is ready to launch. To ensure that the IPP acquisition process is followed, the World Bank has proposed a separate document called the Feasibility Study (annex A2), which presented the status in each of the seven countries regarding the IPP engagement process and the level of readiness for the SMI. In response to the independent TAP on this residual risk, the AE proposed to refine this analysis at the project stage to inform the planned activities.


---
**Page 117**
---

2. The independent TAP believes that the structure of the SRII as described in the funding proposal and annexes, is sufficiently comprehensive to deliver significant solar and wind power capacity additions during and after the project period. This will be facilitated by the availability of concessional financing for renewable energy projects from the Green Climate Fund (GCF) for both solar and wind power at scale, compared to the status quo. This will also remove some of the existing barriers to private sector investment in renewable power capacity.

The provision of guarantees for private sector investments in renewable power capacity will also mitigate the risk associated with the lack of a well-established market for renewable power in these countries. The independent TAP also concurs with the AE that if the process of acquisition by IPPs in each country is open and competitive, they will be able to participate in the SRII without any undue barriers. This will ensure that the private sector can make informed decisions regarding deployment of renewable power projects by the private sector in each of the seven countries.

2.3 Innovation

Innovation is partially delivered through changes to the status quo that result in value creation, reform and renovation of failing systems (inability to deliver sound VRE projects), and ensuring that the SRII intervention process itself is considered innovative.

The development of renewable energy in general and VRE in particular is hampered by various barriers in the seven countries. The proposed SRII intervention is expected to mitigate these barriers and enable the private sector to participate in the deployment of renewable power. To achieve this was presented convincingly in the funding proposal and annexes. Although the technologies (fossil, wind, solar) are not new, the combination of the enabling environment and the transactional may not entirely reflect innovation; however, the SRII intervention process itself can be considered as innovative as its development will include a number of pioneering elements in power systems project countries.

2.3.1 Creation of enabling environment/sustainability of outcomes

The technical process as described above is highly likely to deliver sound VRE outcomes. Activities that will sustain and deliver sound outcomes can be summarised as follows:

(a) Technical assistance to the public sector of the seven countries to enable them to:
(i) Support the public institutions in each country to carry out comprehensive and integrated planning for renewable energy projects, including the development of low-cost generation tools, which will take into consideration, among other things, the integration of VRE with existing power systems. The technical assistance is also expected to build the capacity of key personnel of the relevant public institutions to carry out these planning exercises once the SRII intervention ends;
(ii) The technical assistance will also build the capacity of relevant professionals in public institutions and private sector entities in each country, including by providing transactional advisory support to implement competitive and transparent tendering processes. This will ensure that the public institutions are therefore capable of managing these activities once the SRII expires; and
(iii) The technical support will also deliver technical, environmental, and social studies for wind and solar power future tenders, with robust local capacity-building.

(b) Public investments through a grant mechanism underpinning the SRII process, which will deliver the facilities, which would have been difficult without public sector co-investment in renewable power and especially VRE systems to be become a reality. Key activities that will be covered by this investment include:

The share of RE in their power generation mix by 2018, most countries in Africa and other developing countries were well below 5 per cent: inadequate regulatory frameworks, limited planning, inadequate financial mechanisms, lack of technical expertise, and insufficient access to finance are key barriers identified for scaling up the deployment of renewable energy projects in these countries. # Potential for knowledge-sharing and learning

## The design and implementation plan of the SMI include various strategies for knowledge-sharing and learning built into the SMI programme.

### (a) The use of financing and transaction advisors for the first round of the RE programme is expected to create a cadre of local technical experts within the relevant institutional and regulatory frameworks. These advisors will be able to help countries not only integrate the changes in their energy systems as SMI is implemented into countries long-term.

### (b) The funding proposal section on capacity-building and technology development/transfer states categorically that the strategic analysis included in a comprehensive project document should identify the specific responsibilities of key stakeholders and assess the potential of local entities in relation to their position in the value chain. This will enable the SMI programme to ensure that countries will develop the skills of key stakeholders and improve their chances of benefiting from other initiatives, such as those related to financing and transaction advisors, financed under the SMI, which will support countries through a “learning by doing” process.

### (c) The funding proposal also states that capacity-building will be mainstreamed throughout the RE projects in order to strengthen the countries’ frameworks and develop their institutional capacities for the long-term sustainability of the project (support of transaction advisors). This approach will enable the seven countries to replicate the project effectively.

## The proposed project will deliver comprehensive and innovative attributes that the independent TAP believes will contribute strongly to a high paradigm shift potential. It will also deliver a replicable model for other countries in terms of technology selection, financing, and implementation, while also enhancing replicability of the proof of concept to relevant stakeholders. The development of the knowledge management and training platform under the “learning by doing” process is expected to be an important element.

# Sustainable development potential

## Alignment with the Sustainable Development Goals

The independent TAP found that the project would align well with specific Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs). The independent TAP also concluded that if the SMR is successfully implemented, it will directly contribute to the achievement of the following SDGs:

- **SDG 7**: Ensure access to affordable, reliable, sustainable and modern energy for all.
- **SDG 13**: Take urgent action to combat climate change and its impacts.

The systems planning benefits of SMR will enhance the planning of full infrastructure facilitation, including private IPP investors and providing guarantees that mitigate their perceived risks. In countries with substantial coal power generation and coal resources or traditional energy sources, the SMR will reduce the need for fuel imports and the potential economic impact of fuel cost fluctuations thereby delivering economic co-benefits to the implementing country.

Over time, as a result of the diversification of the project’s energy supply mix, the SMR will also reduce the overall impacts of fossil fuel fluctuations. The funding proposal also states that the SMR process will develop a more resilient and diversified energy supply mix in the countries involved, thereby affecting net fossil fuel imports and delivering further economic co-benefits.

## Economic co-benefits

The systems planning benefits of SMR will enhance the planning of full infrastructure facilitation, including private IPP investors and providing guarantees that mitigate their perceived risks. In countries with substantial coal power generation and coal resources or traditional energy sources, the SMR will reduce the need for fuel imports and the potential economic impact of fuel cost fluctuations thereby delivering economic co-benefits to the implementing country.

Over time, as a result of the diversification of the project’s energy supply mix, the SMR will also reduce the overall impacts of fossil fuel fluctuations. The funding proposal also states that the SMR process will develop a more resilient and diversified energy supply mix in the countries involved, thereby affecting net fossil fuel imports and delivering further economic co-benefits.

## Social and health co-benefits

COVID-19 has resulted in the diversification of a sizeable proportion of fossil fuels available in most countries. The SMR will reduce the overall impacts of fossil fuel fluctuations by changing the project countries, the implementation of SMR will reduce the economic losses that will arise due to more volatile fossil fuel prices.

By leveraging private investment at scale, the SMR will spare limited public sector finance for the countries, which could be directed to other sectors in need such as healthcare and poor households. The SMR will also contribute to improving energy access, health, welfare, and so on. These are co-benefits that will be delivered by a successful implementation of the SMR process.

### Gender-related co-benefits

The gender co-benefit of the proposed SRII were well articulated in the funding proposal and to be included under the Gender Assessment and Action Plan). It was stated that the SRII intervention has been planned to enhance female economic participation in the energy sector and to contribute towards achieving the World Bank Group Gender Strategy (2016-2021). Annex AII details the strategic approach that will be taken by the project team to ensure that gender equality is mainstreamed throughout the project cycle, and ensure the investment projects contribute to promoting female development, for instance through providing women with access to energy services, training opportunities, and employment in and around communities. The two key ways in which gender actions will be implemented were discussed in Annex AII as follows:

At the strategic level a systematic approach will be followed, which will include:
- Support for the projects with a focus on data collection, generating knowledge and providing training and capacity-building activities;
- Utilization of the existing World Bank Energy Sector Management Assistance Program (ESMAP), at every stage of the project cycle, to provide technical assistance to the project team and the client country, especially in areas where gender equality is not being mainstreamed.
- At the facility level, recruitment of a balanced female-to-male employment ratio will be encouraged within the Project Management Unit team to the extent possible; and
- Key stakeholders' buy-in will be encouraged to ensure that the promotion of women's empowerment and enhanced livelihoods are effectively provided with measurable outcomes;

At the project-specific level – the following approach would be followed:
- Data collection to document the baseline situation and identify gaps: this will be integrated into the baseline survey, which is planned for 2018. It will also measure progress during implementation, with a focus on countries where existing baselines are not available or have been outdated.


---
**Page 122**
---

1.4 Needs of the recipient Scale: High

### 1.4.1 Needs of the country

#### 27 Each of the seven countries is susceptible to climate change. As such, they have subscribed to the Paris Agreement and are committed to limiting global temperature rise to below 2°C. A key sector where the greatest contribution can be made by the TAP is energy generation. The deployment of RE systems in these countries will help all other sectors in terms of GHG emission. As stated in many of the important climate change and national planning documents, including the NDCs, implementation of renewable energy projects in these countries would yield tangible results that will contribute to the reduction of carbon emissions and energy security and enable them to contribute to the global aim of limiting temperature increase.

#### 36 Even without the impact of the COVID-19 pandemic on the global economy, in most developing countries (representing the seven SGM countries), barriers exist for the deployment of RE systems. The high cost of RE systems compared with the specific cost of generating power (USD/kWh) from these clean resources has been competitive with the cost of fossil fuel-based generation. However, the pandemic has made it even more difficult to deploy RE systems in these countries as their governments are prioritizing use of public finances for COVID-19 response and recovery measures. The amount of funding available to unlock private investments is therefore no longer available.

#### 35 Furthermore, utilities in these countries are facing tremendous challenges as a result of the ongoing pandemic. The high cost of generation from fossil fuel-based power has also affected their revenues and therefore their financial ratings. Private investors, who are expected to be in line for funding RE systems, have become more risk-averse due to the pandemic. They are now perceived as riskier as the premium on the cost of capital may increase, leading to a rise in the price of debt financing. As such, it is unlikely that any new RE projects will be built until become entrenched and RE systems will not be built at the level envisaged in the countries' NDCs.

#### 38 The SGM has been designed to address these barriers. It is expected to provide funds to meet the funding responsibilities of the seven governments to provide public financing for strengthening their national energy systems, as well as to support the development of stakeholder engagement, which will develop their relevant skill sets for planning energy systems and preparing and implementing robust bid evaluations to select IPPs; strengthen existing


---
**Page 123**
---

### Economic and financial needs of the country

#### 1.4.2.1 Institutional needs

**a.** Institutional needs will be served in each country using grant funding to implement some of the SBIM activities. Specifically, the baseline of relevant public institutions in the power sector is weak: low capacity for project preparation; limited experience with large-scale expansion capacity planning; very low capability to carry out technical analysis for determining how WPPs can contribute to the national energy mix; and a lack of experience with competitive bidding for the selection of IPPs, which requires experience in transaction advisory experiences; and low capacity to plan on the development of RE Park infrastructure. These weaknesses will be resolved through the establishment of an SBIM project management unit (PMU) that will provide transaction advisory team in each lead local national teams in carrying out their duties. As stated in the financial needs section, the PMU will also provide technical assistance and capacity building for these transaction advisory teams to involve building the capacity of the local teams through a “learning by doing” approach. This will be an important step towards institutional strengthening of the SBIM programme, which may then readdress the institutional weaknesses in the baseline.

**b.** The various institutional needs, including those that will benefit the country as a whole and those that are specific to each country, will also be positively affected by the success of the project, as discussed above. In addition, successful implementation and replication after the end of the intervention, will be enhanced as

### 1.5 Country ownership

#### Scale: Medium to high

##### 1.5.1 Evidence of country ownership through participation of relevant stakeholders

- **Annex A7**: Country ownership is strongly related to national stakeholder engagement. Annex A7 of the funding agreement outlines a two-tier stakeholder engagement approach, with the first tier being conducted by the AE in development of the SRIIM concept. According to information available in Annex A7, the AE will use a two-tier approach to stakeholder engagement at facility level and at the project level. The AE will hold a two-day workshop for all seven countries in a comprehensive discussion of the objectives, the components and the important elements of the SRIIM concept.
- **Annex A8**: As part of this facility-level stakeholder engagement, preliminary stakeholder engagements were held in the seven countries with discussions during these engagements on the core objectives and steps of SRIIM, and details on the technical assistance provided by the AE. The AE will continue to engage stakeholders for development of those new projects. This facility-level stakeholder engagement will be continued as soon as GCF funding is approved, as part of each country's project design and implementation as presented in Annex A8. The AE will also conduct a second tier stakeholder engagement analysis, information dissemination, consultation and engagement. The independent TAP noted the strength of prior engagement of the World Bank in these seven nations, and considers that familiarity with the SRIIM concept is likely to be high among relevant stakeholders, which would facilitate their participation.
- **Annex A9**: To provide further evidence of country ownership, a stakeholder analysis will be conducted after each project activity has commenced. A full stakeholder analysis and identification exercise will be conducted for each project. According to the SRIIM Stakeholders Engagement Approach (SEA) (Annex A10), the AE will identify key stakeholders in each country, including potential risks and impacts affecting them and will be carried out for all four project components. The findings of this comprehensive stakeholder analysis will inform the development of the SRIIM concept.
- **Annex A12**: The independent TAP is of the opinion that the outcomes of the second tier stakeholder engagement should be documented in a report to be submitted as part of the SRIIM submission.

### Executing capacity of the executing entity

As stated in the funding proposal, the executing entity (EE) has been carefully chosen to combine the necessary expertise and experience in climate change finance and policy with a strong track record of implementing projects and influencing the country's policies (e.g., the Ministry of Finance). In five of the countries, the national utility was also included as an executing entity. The specific roles and responsibilities for each country are stated in the funding proposal that the purposes of the loans and grants, the EE would be the borrower, while the national utility would act as a co-executing entity, acting through the Ministry of Energy and the state utility, the Botswana Power Corporation. For the purpose of this document, all projects contemplated under Component 1b the EE would be the private sector RE project companies.


---
**Page 126**
---

1. The Ministries of Finance (in CAR, the Ministry of Economy Planning and Cooperation; in Kenya, the National Treasury) are responsible for the management of funds from international institutions on behalf of the Government and ensuring that they are correctly disbursed to the project.
2. The Ministries of Energy in CAR, DRC, Kenya, and Uganda are also involved as co-financing partners and are capable of serving in the capacity of EE. The Ministries of Energy in the participating countries have strong experience in energy sector planning and implementation but limited experience outside this sector. They are not fit for the role in the SRMI programme.
3. In CAR, DRC, Kenya, and Uganda, the Ministry of Energy is likely to seek their participation as a co-financing proposal but the Ministries of Energy are likely to seek their participation. The utility companies have power development experience but in many of the countries, limited RE power experience. This is not an issue for the SRMI programme because it will be implemented through a national tendering process and will train country stakeholders through “learning by doing,” which will provide them with skills not only to participate in the project, but continue to apply their skills in replication projects post SRMI.
4. Based on the analysis presented in 1.5.1, 1.5.2 and 1.5.3 above, the independent TAP considers that the country coverage of this SRMI project submission can be ranked as high.

### 1.6 Efficiency and effectiveness Scale: Medium to high

#### 1.6.1 Financial structure
The proposed project is requesting grant and debt financing from GCF and other co-financing partners. The total amount requested for the project is a combination of grant and co-financing in a total of USD 280 million made up of:
- Grant: USD 54 million, reimbursable grant: USD 30 million
- Co-financing: World Bank co-financing will total USD 1,283 million that will be sourced as follows: grant from the World Bank ESMAP - USD 18 million; grant from the World Bank IDA - USD 60 million; and senior loan from the World Bank IDA - USD 750 million.

The total fund requested from GCF is about 17.9 per cent of total fund required for implementing the seven SBM projects. As a percentage of total SBM programme funding for the seven countries, GCF requested commitments can be summarized as follows:

(a) Grant funding is 3.45 per cent;
(b) Reimbursable grant funding is 1.9 per cent;
(c) Guarantees are 0.76 per cent; and

The grant funding from GCF has been earmarked as follows:

(a) About 37 per cent of the GCF grant funding will be spent to support each of the activities of Component 1 in the five countries, including the preparation of the generation plan with VRE integration and capacity-building in the nine countries.
(b) About 55.6 per cent of GCF grant will be spent on the activities of Component 2 only in CAR (the sixth country), which includes the development of a national grid and park common infrastructure,VRE integration upgrades and grid resilience. These activities in the remaining five countries are funded by loans.
(c) The balance of 7.4 per cent of GCF grant has been earmarked for project management costs.

Reimbursable grant, guarantees and senior loan requested from GCF will be utilized as follows:

(a) The entire USD 176 million senior loan from GCF is planned to be utilized for funding activities of Component 2 in five countries namely: Botswana, Kenya, Mali, Namibia and Zimbabwe.
(b) Activities of Component 2 in CAR (the sixth country) will be financed by grant from GCF;
(c) The reimbursable grant of USD 30 million has been entirely earmarked to provide a risk mitigation instrument for mini-grids in the five countries;
(d) The USD 20 million guarantee funding from GCF will be spent in equal proportion to provide risk mitigation instruments for mini-grids in Botswana and Namibia.

### Co-financing, leveraging and mobilizing long-term investment

The USD 200 million requested from GCF will be complemented by a total USD 1.283 billion of co-financing from the World Bank Group. The World Bank fund are expected to be public investments in the electricity sectors of the countries, which will enable leveraging and mobilizing long-term investment for the SBM programme. Implementation of these public investments is expected to leverage around USD 3 billion in private sector investments (World Bank and GCF financing) and private investments of about 1 to 2.4. This can be considered an effective and efficient use of funds of the SBM programme. As stated in the financial plan, the World Bank Group co-financing is a long-term investment as it is successful as laid out in the project components. Therefore, the leveraged USD 3 billion enables mobilizing long-term investments.

The independent TAP of the is that this financial leveraging ratio indicates sound cost-effectiveness of the project when compared with benchmarks of similar projects recently funded by GCF


---
**Page 128**
---

1. **Cost efficiency**

   * The project is expected to deliver about 16.1 MtCO2eq of GHG emission reductions over a 20-year period. The efficiency metrics that will be delivered by this project intervention were estimated as follows:
     - (a) Total project financing = USD 1,523 million
     - (b) Required co-financing = USD 289 million
     - (c) Expected lifetime GHG emissions = 880,780,000 tCO2eq
     - (d) Estimated cost per tonCO2eq (a/c) = USD 17.57/tCO2eq
     - (e) Estimated GCF cost per tonCO2eq (b/c) = USD 3.15/tCO2eq

   * The independent TAP agrees with the conclusion in the funding proposal that the estimated lifetime GHG emission reductions are as shown above, when added to the project's additional benefits (better electricity access, resilience of power infrastructure, etc.) represent a significant contribution to GCF’s climate change mitigation objective. The lifetime reduction cost is compared to estimations for other similar energy projects already approved and funded by GCF.

2. **Economic and financial rate of return of the investment**

   * The independent TAP reviewed the economic and financial analysis carried out for the programme, which was prepared in accordance with the guidelines provided by the GCF Secretariat (see annex A3 – SRI: Economic and Financial – 30 November 2020) and came to the following conclusions:
     - (a) Results for all the countries analysed showed that GCF financing increased the financial internal rate of return (IRR) by an average of about 2 per cent. The concessional GCF financing provided a significant contribution towards achieving higher project IRRs than market rates; and
     - (b) The financial analysis conducted for five of the countries, namely Botswana, Kenya, Mali, Namibia, and Zimbabwe for activities under Components 1 and 2, yielded financial IRR of 13 per cent, 27 per cent, 27 per cent, 12 per cent and 14 per cent respectively. The concessional GCF financing provided by the Fund would be instrumental in achieving an acceptable IRR for investment in these countries.
     - (c) The independent TAP therefore concluded that the efficiency and effectiveness of implementation of this project will be underpinned by financial returns.

3. **Overall remarks from the independent Technical Advisory Panel**

   * The independent TAP further recommended that the Board approve the SRI:
     - (a) Provided that the TAP further recommends to the Board that, if the funding proposal is approved, the AE shall agree to the inclusion of the following reporting requirements in the funded activity agreement: to be signed between GCF and the AE;
     - (b) Provided under Component 2, the AE will provide a report at the Facility level, also covering Project-level activities information; and as when available, at the minimum coverage required by GCF:
       * report detailing the success and challenges of the gender mainstreaming process presented in the Gender Action Plan;

## Paradigm shift potential
